The pi application uses a black background with amber explanatory text and green formulas. A white square plotting region is divided by thin horizontal and vertical axes.

The visible explanation describes a Monte Carlo dart-throwing simulation. Points are placed randomly within a square and categorized according to whether they fall within a centered circular region. The interface provides a formula based on four times the proportion of points inside that circle.

Initially, the remaining-seconds value is 3,600, and both pixel counters are zero. A start control appears above the plotting region.

The document is a JavaScript program that implements a browser-based Monte Carlo simulation for approximating pi. It generates random points, classifies them according to whether they fall inside a circle inscribed within a square, draws them on an HTML canvas, counts the two categories, and continually recalculates an estimate of pi. It also manages the application’s initialization, countdown, button visibility, and timestamped event logging.

The opening comment identifies the file as pi_approximation.js, attributes authorship to karbytes, gives December 29, 2024, as its date, and declares PUBLIC_DOMAIN as its license. The file contains twenty-five named functions. Extensive comments explain their purposes, expected arguments, return values, assumptions, and relationships to the corresponding webpage.

The program is designed to operate within a companion HTML document. It expects a canvas, four numerical readouts, two buttons, and a timestamped-event container to exist under specific element identifiers. Its functions retrieve and modify these elements through the browser’s document interface. The JavaScript provides behavior, while the surrounding webpage supplies the elements on which that behavior depends.

The file itself consists of function definitions. Loading it makes those functions available; initialization and starting are requested through calls from the surrounding page. The two principal entry points are initialize_application(), which prepares the interface, and start_monte_carlo_simulation(), which begins a timed run.

The mathematical intention is to estimate pi from the relationship between a circle and its enclosing square. The square is modeled with coordinates extending from negative 200 to positive 200 along each axis. A circle centered at the origin has a radius of 200. Points whose distance from the origin is no greater than 200 belong to the circle and are counted as red. Points farther away are counted as blue.

For a continuously and uniformly sampled square, the fraction of points inside the circle would approach the circle’s share of the square’s area. That share is pi divided by four. Multiplying the observed red fraction by four therefore supplies the familiar Monte Carlo estimate.

The program expresses this calculation as four times the red count divided by the sum of the red and blue counts. Every displayed estimate is derived from those accumulated counts. The program does not insert a predetermined final value of pi into the readout.

The first function, generate_random_coordinate_scalar(), produces one coordinate. It begins by multiplying Math.random() by 201 and rounding downward, generating an integer magnitude from zero through 200. It then makes another random choice to select a sign. That sign comes from generating an integer from zero through ninety-nine and testing whether it is even or odd. Even results produce a positive sign, and odd results produce a negative sign.

The magnitude is multiplied by the selected sign. The returned coordinate is consequently an integer between negative 200 and positive 200, inclusive. Positive and negative nonzero coordinates are treated symmetrically.

The next function, generate_random_planar_point(), calls the scalar generator separately for the horizontal and vertical coordinates. It returns an object with properties named x_coordinate and y_coordinate. Each point therefore requires four calls to Math.random(): one magnitude choice and one sign choice for each coordinate.

An important detail of this procedure is that the possible numerical coordinates do not all have equal probability. Zero can result from either sign applied to a zero magnitude. Each particular nonzero coordinate requires one specific magnitude and one specific sign.

Assuming the random choices behave uniformly, zero therefore has probability one divided by 201, while each particular nonzero coordinate has probability one divided by 402. Zero is twice as likely as any individual nonzero coordinate. The generator remains symmetric around the origin, but its distribution gives additional weight to points on the coordinate axes.

The point generator also produces a finite integer lattice. There are 401 distinct numerical coordinates available along each axis, giving 160,801 distinct coordinate pairs. This is a discrete sampling model whose exact probabilities differ from the ideal continuous-area model used to motivate the experiment.

The function is_point() validates the point representation. It requires exactly one argument, checks that both coordinate properties are numbers, verifies that both are integers, and checks that each falls within the permitted range from negative 200 through positive 200.

A valid point returns true. An invalid point causes an explanatory message to be written to the browser console and returns false. Missing coordinates, fractional coordinates, out-of-range values, and unsuitable inputs therefore have a defined validation route. The function checks the required coordinate properties without requiring that they be the object’s only properties.

The distance calculation is implemented through two functions. One approximates a square root, and the other uses that operation within the Euclidean distance formula.

The square-root function, approximate_square_root(), uses an iterative averaging procedure. It accepts one argument, checks that the argument is a number, and rejects negative values. It then initializes one working value to the input and another to one.

During each iteration, the first working value becomes the average of the previous two values. The second becomes the original input divided by the newly calculated first value. For the positive integer inputs relevant to the program, these values converge toward the square root.

The stopping condition uses a fixed precision-control value of 0.000000001. Iteration continues while the difference between the two working values exceeds that threshold. The function returns the first working value when the loop ends.

This is an implementation of the averaging approach commonly associated with Heron’s method for square roots. It allows the program to expose an intermediate numerical algorithm inside the larger simulation instead of relying on the built-in square-root operation.

The function’s documented input assumption is a nonnegative integer. That assumption matches the squared-coordinate sums generated by the distance calculation. The broader numerical validation is less restrictive than the comment: fractional numbers are not explicitly rejected.

That distinction matters for inputs outside the intended application. For example, an input of 0.25 returns 0.25 rather than 0.5 because the initial difference fails the loop condition. Zero, however, returns zero correctly. These behaviors show that the function is suited to its intended nonnegative-integer use more narrowly than its number-type check alone might suggest.

The distance function, compute_distance_between_two_planar_points(), requires exactly two valid point objects. It subtracts their horizontal coordinates and their vertical coordinates, squares both differences, adds the results, and passes the sum to approximate_square_root().

The returned value represents the distance between the two points. During dart classification, one point is the generated sample and the other is the origin. The general function nevertheless accepts any two valid points in the permitted coordinate range.

If validation or another operation fails, the distance function reports the exception to the console and returns zero. This is a fallback value rather than a separate error object, so a caller must understand that zero can represent either a genuine zero distance or the function’s error response.

The function convert_point_to_pixel() translates the mathematical coordinate system into the canvas coordinate system. In the mathematical model, the origin is at the square’s center, positive horizontal values extend rightward, and positive vertical values extend upward. On an HTML canvas, the origin is at the upper-left corner, with vertical coordinates increasing downward.

The conversion adds 200 to the mathematical horizontal coordinate and subtracts the mathematical vertical coordinate from 200. Thus, the mathematical origin becomes canvas position 200, 200. The mathematical upper-left corner becomes canvas position zero, zero. The mathematical lower-right corner becomes canvas position 400, 400.

The vertical subtraction reverses the direction of the vertical axis, preserving the intended geometric orientation. This function is the bridge between abstract coordinate objects and visible drawing positions.

The central plotting function is plot_point_on_html_canvas(). It first checks its input with is_point(). If the supplied value is invalid, it substitutes the origin. This permits the routine to continue, although it means an invalid supplied point becomes an inside-circle sample.

The function calculates the point’s distance from the origin, converts its coordinates to a canvas position, retrieves the canvas, and obtains its two-dimensional drawing context.

If the distance exceeds 200, the function draws a blue mark and increments the blue count. If the distance is no greater than 200, it draws a red mark and increments the red count. Points exactly on the boundary are therefore assigned to the red category.

For both colors, the drawing operation begins a path, creates a rectangle with width one and height one at the converted position, assigns a stroke color, and strokes the path. The blue stroke uses the standard hexadecimal value for pure blue, and the red stroke uses the corresponding value for pure red.

The implementation uses outlined rectangles rather than a dedicated filled-pixel operation. Each mark has a nominal one-by-one canvas-unit rectangle, while its visible rasterization depends on stroke placement and canvas rendering. The counters consequently represent classified dart events rather than a measurement of uniquely colored display pixels.

Repeated coordinates can occur, allowing later marks to overlap earlier marks. A repeated dart still increases a counter even when it adds little or no distinguishable new area to the image. The canvas accumulates the graphical record, while the numerical fields accumulate the number of sampling events.

After drawing and counting a point, the plotting function calls update_pi_approximation(). Each successful dart therefore produces an immediate numerical update.

This design makes the displayed HTML values part of the program’s working state. The counts are repeatedly read from the document rather than maintained exclusively in separate JavaScript variables. The interface serves both as a presentation layer and as a store for values used by subsequent calculations.

The counter helpers assume values between zero and 3,600. Under the intended run structure, the two category counts together reach 3,600 because exactly one point is classified during each scheduled step.

The function update_pi_approximation() retrieves both counts, computes four times the red fraction, and writes the result into the pi readout. It does not apply explicit decimal rounding. The displayed numerical representation therefore comes from JavaScript’s ordinary conversion of the calculated number into text.

An initial estimate of zero is assigned during initialization. That zero is a starting display value. If the update function were called while both category counts were zero, its expression would divide zero by zero and produce NaN, meaning “Not a Number.” Initialization avoids that operation by directly assigning zero.

After the first actual dart, the calculated estimate must be either four or zero. A red first dart gives a red fraction of one, while a blue first dart gives a red fraction of zero. The estimate becomes more informative as additional samples accumulate.

Two functions manage the countdown. get_seconds_remaining() reads the remaining-time span and returns its parsed value. decrement_seconds_remaining() reads that value, subtracts one, and writes the result back. Both include checks against the intended zero-to-3,600 range.

The countdown represents scheduled simulation steps. It begins at 3,600 and decreases once per timer callback. Its normal relationship to the counters is that the red count plus the blue count equals 3,600 minus the remaining count.

That relationship follows from the callback’s sequence: each step decrements the countdown and plots one newly generated point. An isolated execution check confirmed this normal lifecycle through all 3,600 steps.

These lines intersect at the canvas center. They provide a visual reference for the Cartesian origin and divide the square into four quadrants.

It then calls initialize_application(). Starting a run therefore resets the canvas, countdown, and numerical counters before scheduling any darts. This also generates another initialization entry immediately after the start entry.

That order explains why a normally used page can contain an initial page-load initialization message, followed later by a start message and another initialization message. These entries correspond to the code’s explicit sequence.

After initialization, the start function hides START and creates an interval through setInterval(). The requested interval is 1,000 milliseconds. Its callback calls plot_random_pixel_on_square_canvas() and passes along the interval handle.

Because initialization has already hidden RESET, both controls are absent during the normal run. The first dart is scheduled for the first interval callback rather than being plotted immediately within the start function.

The function plot_random_pixel_on_square_canvas() performs each timed step. It generates a random point, decrements the countdown, retrieves the updated remaining value, and plots the point. Plotting includes classification, drawing, counting, and recalculating the estimate.

Enumerating the integer-coordinate outcomes classified inside or on the radius-200 circle gives an inside weight of 126,432. Four times that inside fraction is approximately 3.1294274894.

This is a mathematical inference from the source’s sampling rules, rather than a value displayed or stated by the program. It means that finite-sample fluctuations occur around a slightly shifted expectation produced by the discrete, weighted coordinate distribution.

The first stage of the implementation generates coordinates. generate_random_coordinate_scalar() chooses an integer magnitude from zero through 200, then separately chooses a positive or negative sign. generate_random_planar_point() invokes that process twice and returns an object containing horizontal and vertical coordinate properties.

The resulting coordinates lie between negative 200 and positive 200, inclusive. Their representation places the mathematical origin at the center of the modeled square.

The function is_point() validates this representation. It checks that the expected coordinate properties are numbers, that they are integers, and that they fall within the permitted range. Invalid inputs produce explanatory console messages and a false result.

The program computes distance using a separate function that subtracts coordinates, squares the differences, adds them, and takes an approximate square root. This exposes the ordinary Euclidean distance formula directly in the implementation.

The square-root operation is implemented through an iterative averaging method. One working value begins at the input and another at one. The function repeatedly replaces one with their average and the other with the original input divided by that average. It stops when the difference between the working values falls below a fixed precision threshold.

The threshold is one billionth. For the nonnegative integer inputs generated by the program’s squared-coordinate sums, this procedure supplies the distance approximation used in classification.

The function convert_point_to_pixel() translates mathematical coordinates into canvas coordinates. It adds 200 to the horizontal coordinate and subtracts the vertical coordinate from 200. The mathematical origin consequently maps to the canvas center at position 200, 200.

The vertical subtraction accounts for the difference between a Cartesian plane, where positive vertical values extend upward, and a canvas, where vertical positions increase downward from the upper-left corner.

The main plotting routine validates its input, calculates the distance from the origin, converts the position, and obtains the canvas’s two-dimensional drawing context. Distances greater than 200 produce blue marks. Distances no greater than 200 produce red marks, including points exactly on the boundary.

Each mark is drawn as a stroked rectangle with nominal width and height of one canvas unit. The appropriate color counter is incremented, and the approximation is recalculated.

The counters record sampling events. Repeated coordinates can cause marks to overlap, so the number of darts counted need not equal the number of separately distinguishable colored locations in the image.

Counter helpers read numerical values from the webpage, parse them as integers, increment or retrieve them, and write updated values back. The displayed spans therefore serve as part of the program’s working state.

update_pi_approximation() retrieves both counts and calculates four times the red fraction. It writes the resulting JavaScript number directly into the approximation readout without an explicit rounding step.

The long decimal representation visible in the screenshots consequently reflects the arithmetic result’s formatting. It does not establish an equally large number of accurate digits of pi. Statistical accuracy depends on the sampling model and accumulated outcomes.

Other helpers retrieve and decrement the countdown. Two drawing functions create horizontal and vertical black axes through the canvas center. clear_canvas() removes existing drawing content so the axes and samples can be rebuilt for another run.

The document is a complete HTML application that monitors a fixed onion webpage by making an immediate retrieval attempt and then requesting another attempt every sixty seconds. It displays cumulative attempt, success, and failure counts, calculates a success percentage, and retains a table containing the most recent one hundred completed results. It also provides an optional control that starts three looping audio recordings, intended to help keep the browser session active when the page moves into the background on a mobile device.

The opening source comment identifies the file as onion_web_page_availability_logger.html, attributes authorship to karbytes, gives May 5, 2025, as its date, and declares PUBLIC_DOMAIN as its license. The file contains its interface, stylesheet, and JavaScript together. Its operational dependencies are the monitored onion destination, the externally hosted audio files, and the browser environment through which those resources are accessed.

The browser title is “.onion Fetch Monitor (with Pruning),” while the visible heading is “.onion Page Fetch Monitor.” The additional wording in the browser title identifies an important implementation feature: the application removes older table entries as new results arrive, maintaining a bounded visible history.

The introductory text describes the application as determining whether a particular onion webpage is discoverable at a particular time. Its concrete operation is repeated fetching of a known destination. The program does not search for unknown pages or examine whether a search engine has indexed the target.

A second paragraph states that the application logs the most recent one hundred polling instances, with one poll every sixty seconds, until the browser window is closed. This introduces the nominal cadence, the history limit, and the session-based operating period before the implementation is presented.

Two parenthesized notes describe the intended deployment environment. One states that the monitoring application must be hosted from a dark-web server. The other states that it must be run through a dark-web browser such as Tor. The source supplies these as operating instructions; it contains no code that creates an onion service, configures routing, or verifies that the visitor is using Tor.

The page distinguishes the monitored destination from the monitoring application itself. The target is the root page of an onion website. The monitoring application occupies a nested location under that same website. A source comment explicitly warns that the address being polled is not the address of the page performing the polling.

This distinction matters because the program tests retrieval of the selected root page rather than repeatedly fetching another copy of its own interface. The audio files are stored elsewhere beneath the same onion host, within the directory associated with a sound-looping application.

The interface includes a documentation reference leading to an explanatory page on Karbytes For Life Blog. A yellow background emphasizes this reference. A cyan-highlighted paragraph separately identifies the configured target and repeats the sixty-second interval.

The target description is static text in the HTML, while the executable target is assigned separately in JavaScript. The application does not generate all its displayed configuration text from a single variable. Its current source keeps the displayed description and executable setting consistent, but they remain distinct parts of the document.

Below the polling description, an italicized paragraph introduces the session-maintenance mechanism. It states that a script can halt when the page is no longer the active application window on a mobile device and invites the visitor to start playing sound tracks with staggered durations.

The associated button is labeled keep_session_active(). This function-style label makes its operational purpose explicit. The button is the page’s principal user-operated control; monitoring itself begins automatically.

The internal stylesheet gives the body a sans-serif font and twenty pixels of padding. The results table occupies the available width, uses collapsed borders, and has a twenty-pixel top margin. Header and data cells receive dark borders, eight pixels of padding, and left-aligned text. Header cells have a light-gray background.

The statistics region receives a small top margin and bold lettering. Its four readouts are Total Attempts, Successes, Failures, and Success Rate.

The session-maintenance button has a bright-green background and border, black text, rounded corners, ten pixels of padding, and sixteen-pixel monospaced lettering. Its normal browser appearance is suppressed. On hover, its background and border become cyan.

These presentation rules give the application a straightforward reporting layout. Explanations and configuration appear first, the optional audio control follows, the aggregate statistics come next, and the detailed table occupies the lower portion.

The initial HTML assigns zero to all three counters and “0%” to the success-rate field. The table body begins empty. These are starting values, not measurements preserved from an earlier run.

The table has four columns: ISO Timestamp, Unix Epoch (ms), Status, and Notes. This structure combines two representations of time with a short outcome category and a more descriptive annotation.

The embedded JavaScript implements five named functions: startPlayingSilentSoundTracks(), keep_session_active(), updateStats(), logResult(), and visitPage(). It also creates the audio objects, declares the monitoring configuration and counters, makes the initial polling call, and schedules subsequent calls.

The monitoring configuration is fixed in the source. targetUrl identifies the onion root page, intervalMilliseconds is sixty multiplied by one thousand, and maxRows is one hundred. The interface provides no editable fields for selecting another destination, changing the interval, or changing the retained-history limit.

Three mutable variables hold the aggregate state: totalAttempts, successCount, and failureCount. All begin at zero when the script is executed for a new page load.

The program’s placement near the end of the HTML body means the elements it needs have already been declared when monitoring begins. It calls visitPage() immediately and then passes that function to setInterval() with the sixty-thousand-millisecond interval.

The first attempt therefore starts without waiting for the first minute to elapse. Later attempts are requested on the recurring schedule. The page does not require the visitor to press the audio button before it begins monitoring.

Each visitPage() call first creates a Date object. From that same object, it obtains an ISO-formatted timestamp through toISOString() and a numerical timestamp through getTime(). These two values represent the same recorded instant in different forms.

Their placement before the network operation establishes an important interpretation: the logged timestamp records the beginning of the attempt, rather than the moment its response or error becomes available.

One source comment incorrectly describes the Unix epoch as beginning on January 1, 1990. JavaScript’s getTime() uses the standard epoch beginning on January 1, 1970. The actual numerical timestamps therefore follow the ordinary 1970-based convention; the incorrect year is confined to the explanatory comment.

After capturing the time, visitPage() increments totalAttempts. It then enters a try block and awaits a fetch request.

The request uses the GET method, specifies no-cors mode, and requests no-store cache handling. It also appends the numerical timestamp as a query parameter to the target address. This gives successive attempts ordinarily distinct request addresses and expresses the application’s intention to obtain fresh retrieval observations.

The timestamp parameter is used for request variation, not as a response-time measurement. The program does not record a second timestamp after the request completes or calculate latency from a start-and-finish pair.

If fetch returns a fulfilled response, successCount is incremented. The program then appends a row whose status is “Attempted” and whose note identifies the response type.

The use of “Attempted” is significant. A result counted as successful is not labeled “Successful” in the table. Its interpretation comes from the fulfilled-fetch branch, the response-type note, and the aggregate success counter.

The response type is recorded through response.type. The code does not restrict success to one particular type. It also does not examine response.ok, inspect the numerical HTTP status, read the response body, or verify that the returned page contains expected content.

Consequently, success has a specific operational meaning in this program: the fetch operation fulfilled with a response object. A fulfilled response carrying an error HTTP status would still follow the success branch. An opaque response would also be counted if the fetch operation fulfilled with it.

The no-cors option therefore needs to be understood alongside the limited inspection performed by the program. The script records the response classification available to it, but it does not establish that the destination returned the intended document with a successful HTTP status.

If the try block throws, failureCount is incremented. The program appends a row labeled “Failed” and uses error.toString() as the note. This preserves an error’s textual description, including its type when that is part of the string.

The failure category formally covers exceptions caught within that block. Because row logging also occurs inside the try block, an exception while writing a successful row could enter the failure branch too. Under the intended interface structure, the normal distinction is between fulfilled requests and rejected requests, but the catch is not restricted solely to network exceptions.

After either normal outcome branch, visitPage() calls updateStats(). This refreshes the displayed counters and success percentage.

updateStats() assigns the current values through textContent. For a nonzero attempt count, it divides successes by total attempts, multiplies by one hundred, rounds the displayed result to two decimal places, and appends a percent sign. When there have been no attempts, it uses “0%.”

The denominator is all attempts started, not only attempts that have finished. This becomes important when requests overlap or remain pending.

totalAttempts increases before the awaited fetch completes, but the visible statistics are refreshed only after a result or error has been handled. While the first request is pending, the internal attempt count can already be one even though the initial displayed counter remains zero.

If another attempt starts before an earlier one finishes, a later completion can refresh the interface while some requests are still pending. The displayed total can then exceed the sum of completed successes and failures.

For example, two attempts may have started while only one has returned successfully. At that point, the refreshed display can show two attempts, one success, no failures, and a fifty-percent success rate. The missing outcome is pending rather than necessarily erroneous.

This also means that the success percentage can change because the denominator includes unfinished attempts. It is a ratio based on the program’s current counters, not a direct measurement of continuous uptime.

The recurring interval does not wait for the preceding visitPage() call to finish. setInterval() invokes the asynchronous function at its requested cadence without using the previous promise’s completion as a scheduling condition.

The source contains no request timeout, abort operation, or in-progress flag. A sufficiently slow request can therefore coexist with subsequent requests. The program also does not adjust its interval in response to failures or apply a backoff schedule.

The interval handle is not retained, and the file defines no stop-monitoring function. Its stated operating lifetime ends with the browser session or page context rather than through an on-page stop control.

The logResult() function manages the visible history. It finds the table body and checks how many data rows it contains. When the count is already at least one hundred, it deletes the first row before appending the new result.

The header is outside the table body, so the one-hundred-row limit applies to data entries. Starting from an empty body, the normal procedure retains no more than one hundred result rows.

Each new row is created explicitly. The function creates a table-row element, then creates four data cells for the ISO time, numerical time, status, and note. It assigns each value through textContent and appends the row to the table body.

Using textContent makes the values displayed text. The notes are not deliberately interpreted as HTML markup when inserted into the table.

Rows are added only after the result of a request is known, but they carry timestamps captured before the request began. The insertion order therefore follows completion order, while the timestamp values describe attempt-start times.

If requests finish out of order, the table can contain a later start time above an earlier start time. The function does not sort rows chronologically after insertion.

This affects the exact meaning of pruning. The deleted row is the first retained row in insertion order. It is the oldest retained completion entry, which need not always be the earliest attempt timestamp when requests overlap.

The history limit and aggregate counters operate independently. Deleting a row does not reduce totalAttempts, successCount, or failureCount. Those counters cover the entire current page session, while the table retains only the bounded recent history.

A failure can consequently disappear from the table after enough later results arrive while remaining represented in the failure count and success rate. A table containing only successful-looking retained rows can coexist with a cumulative success percentage below one hundred.

The program keeps no separate complete array of all historical results and defines no persistent-storage or export procedure. Once a row is pruned, the application’s own retained representation of that individual timestamp and note is gone. Its aggregate contribution remains in the counters.

Under regular, non-overlapping one-minute polling, one hundred observations would span ninety-nine intervals between the first and last attempt times, or approximately one hour and thirty-nine minutes. Actual retained coverage can differ because of scheduling delays, pending requests, and completion order.

The other major part of the source is its optional audio mechanism. A long comment explains the author’s motivation: a browser tab may become dormant when treated as a background process on a mobile device, and continuing audio playback is intended to help preserve activity.

The comment contrasts mobile foreground limitations with the ability to keep several applications or tabs active on a laptop. This is explanatory motivation for the design. The code itself does not control operating-system scheduling or guarantee that a browser will remain active indefinitely.

The sound-track list contains three MP3 references. They identify a karbytes drum recording dated October 20, 2023, a frog-croaking recording from Castro Valley dated April 21, 2022, and a karbytes guitar recording dated December 12, 2022.

Comments give their durations as two hundred seconds, sixteen seconds, and one hundred nine seconds, respectively. These durations are descriptive annotations. The program does not measure the files’ actual lengths or enforce those values.

The script maps the three references into three Audio objects. Each object has looping enabled. Each also receives a volume value of one, representing full volume at the audio-element level.

A commented-out line shows an earlier or alternative setting of zero volume. The active line uses full volume, and its comment explains that the author found muted playback insufficient for the intended session-maintenance purpose.

This produces a conspicuous naming mismatch. The function startPlayingSilentSoundTracks() retains “Silent” in its name, but the configured recordings are not muted by the code. The source comments explicitly acknowledge that the current configuration is audible.

The comments also propose a possible variation: replacing the recordings with genuinely silent audio files while leaving their volume controls at full level. That proposal distinguishes a silent signal from a muted audio element. It is described as a modification someone could make, not an implemented or verified feature of this file.

startPlayingSilentSoundTracks() iterates over the existing Audio objects and calls play() on each. Each playback promise has its own rejection handler, which sends a warning to the browser console if playback fails.

The separate handlers allow each requested track to have its own playback outcome. The function does not update the monitoring table or the success and failure counters when an audio request fails. Those reporting mechanisms belong to the webpage-fetching process.

The function keep_session_active() is a small wrapper that calls the playback helper. The button’s click handler invokes this wrapper. Its name expresses the intended purpose while the helper performs the audio operations.

Placing playback behind a user action is deliberate. The comments cite restrictions on starting ongoing audio without user interaction. The code provides that interaction through the visible button rather than attempting to start the recordings automatically with monitoring.

The three Audio objects are created once. Repeated button clicks reuse them rather than constructing a new set of players on every click. The source does not reset their playback positions or provide a dedicated stop, pause, mute, or volume-selection interface.

The explanatory text calls the recordings’ durations staggered. In the implementation, their playback is requested together without explicitly scheduled start offsets. Their different lengths cause their loop boundaries to occur at different times during much of the session.

The comments describe this as a way to avoid gaps in ongoing audio activity. Different durations alone do not enforce uninterrupted playback, and the code contains no crossfading, gap measurement, or coordinated boundary scheduling. The distinction is between the author’s intended effect and the precise operations implemented.

A commented-out call to the playback helper appears inside visitPage(). Because that line is inactive, the automatic polling routine does not start the sound tracks. The button remains the active route to requesting playback.

Another comment explains why the audio-related definitions precede the monitoring code, referring specifically to that commented-out call. This preserves some of the implementation’s development history: earlier possibilities and later adjustments remain visible within the source.

The monitoring and audio functions are therefore related operationally but remain separate in execution. Polling begins automatically. Audio begins through the button. Audio is intended to support continued browser activity, while the fetch routine performs the actual observations.

The HTML provides no independent confirmation that audio playback has successfully kept a session active. It also provides no audio-status readout. Playback problems are reported through console warnings rather than through a visible state indicator.

The application’s data likewise remain local to the current page’s execution state. Its counters are JavaScript variables, and its recent records are table rows. A fresh execution initializes them again. The source contains no mechanism for restoring an earlier session’s results.

Its date, counters, timestamps, and titles consequently have different roles. The May 2025 header is source metadata. The initial zeros describe startup state. Runtime timestamps record when attempts begin. The success percentage summarizes the program’s current interpretation of its accumulated requests.

The program’s strongest measurements are the individual attempt-start times, whether the relevant code path obtained a fulfilled response or caught an exception, and the response-type or error text it records. These observations can help document reachability from the monitoring environment.

Their interpretation remains narrower than a complete service-health assessment. The source does not verify expected page content, certify successful HTTP status, measure response latency, or observe conditions continuously between polls.

The total-attempt counter increases before the request finishes, while the display is refreshed after an outcome. Pending requests can therefore affect the relationship among the counters.

If several requests overlap, the total number started can exceed the sum of completed successes and failures. A refreshed success percentage can include unfinished attempts in its denominator. ## My reading of the video

I keep coming back to the fact that the first thing I can read is “karbytes is stuck,” and the last intelligible words are “Onion’s back online.” They sit at opposite ends of the recording without an explicit connection. In the first image, a person and a geometric object are combined into a single figure. In the final sequence, a set of service commands and a browser request produce a small page on a laptop. The words “stuck” and “back” give those two events a relationship in my reading, even though the recording leaves their relationship open. The collage gives its figure a physical predicament, with hands resting on the shape that surrounds it. The spoken phrase gives a piece of software a kind of whereabouts. It has come back.

I find the first minute especially important because it gives the final phrase a place to land. The camera could have stayed close to the laptop from the start. Instead it passes a window or door, framed pictures, chairs, storage, and a tray of lights. At one point I can see branches against the blue sky. At another the camera is so low that the carpet and a loose cable become prominent. The eventual view of a page coming through Tor belongs to that same place. The last shot makes the relationship visible again by showing how small the screen is in the room and what it is physically resting on.

The changing distance from the display also changes what holds my attention. Close up, I look for words, a command, a status line, or the cursor. As the camera backs away, the visible text dissolves and I notice the shape of the keyboard, the case, and the wall. Even in the closest views, the oblique angle and the marks across the display keep drawing my attention back to the screen as a physical surface. I find the blank white interval just before the first onion page particularly effective in this respect. For a few seconds the browser offers almost nothing to read, and those little marks are the most distinct things in the picture. Then the orange heading and cyan passage arrive all at once.

The long terminal section gives the end its pace. I can see the routine of entering a line, waiting, and getting back a different kind of text. The waits occupy actual screen time. The browser repeats that rhythm later, first with the connection page, then with the pause before the directory, and again with the white page before the destination. I find myself watching for small changes because the recording has taught me that they matter. A shift in the page’s color can register the outcome of a whole stretch of work whose other signs were a cursor and a few lines of tiny text.

The brief view of a face and the final speech put the person into the recording in two different ways. The face appears before the terminal work, close to the camera and partly lit from below. The words arrive after the work, when the camera has turned away from the close view of the page. I read the remark as a compact acknowledgment of what has just appeared. The word “back” also suggests a history that the clip begins too late to show. I’m left with the duration of this attempt, the visible fact of a page loading in that browser, and the operator’s own short description of the result.

My strongest impression of the ending is of the page becoming small again. For several seconds its appearance has been the event I am watching for. By the last frame, it is one illuminated patch in a room with other light sources, furniture, shadows, and cables. The recording ends while the machine is still there doing whatever follows a successful load. That last change of scale gives me room to think about the rest of the activity that the clip only briefly makes visible.

## Voice and soundtrack

The recoverable narration appears to be one voice speaking from behind the camera. The pitch tracker puts its median voiced fundamental near **104 Hz**, with the middle 80 percent of tracked values between roughly **85 and 128 Hz**. It is a low-register delivery with enough movement to distinguish an emphasized instruction from a phrase winding down. The pitch rises on parts of “Just press the button.” In “this beautiful morning,” it descends across the phrase, from around 118 Hz near the start of that passage toward the upper 80s at the end. The rhythm is conversational and segmented. Some clauses come in short groups, with gaps of a second or more while the picture changes or the page scrolls. The π explanation is the most sustained stretch.

The signal has pronounced low and lower-mid harmonics. In several clear speech excerpts, about 90 percent of the measured energy falls between 80 Hz and 1 kHz. That supports an impression of a full, low-centered resonance, with short higher-frequency bursts separating the sustained voiced sounds. Finer timbral descriptions would require direct listening. The speech windows average about −25 dBFS in the mono analysis, well above the repeating background in most neighboring gaps, which is often around −38 to −44 dBFS. The final “karbytes in its burrow” is several decibels softer than the preceding sentence about the morning. These are levels in the recording.

The early terminal section has a low, relatively steady background with intermittent short transients. Around 2:29, the signal becomes more rhythmic and substantially stronger as the looper starts. Spectral analysis shows recurring attacks with a spacing of about 0.35 seconds in the first passage, and a different repeating pattern near 0.41 seconds in later gaps in the narration. The repetitive signal is consistent with music or rhythmic audio playback, and the first change aligns closely with the on-screen control. I could not identify the instrumentation or assign every later sound to that same playback source. Once the camera is outside, the sustained level drops again. The final section has mostly low-level sound and occasional sharper peaks, with no further intelligible narration recovered.

The philosophical pages give the transcript a vocabulary for considering such wholes and sequences. The static block multiverse model describes nature at its largest scale as timelessly complete. Within that whole, observers experience particular universes as growing, with a history that seems to gain temporal length and information and may appear to gain volume or energy. A phenomenon is a finite allocation of space, time, energy, and sometimes matter rendered as a distinguishable appearance in a frame of reference. Its corresponding noumenon encompasses possible configurations. A noumenon may also lack a phenomenal counterpart. The document uses a variable capable of holding both zero and one as an example of alternatives belonging to one abstract representation, while a particular rendering presents a distinguishable configuration. A frame is a small bounded rendering, a timeline is a chronological succession of frames, and a meta-frame is the apparently persisting present experienced by the agent. An image of nested, green-lit cubic forms with glowing edges illustrates the relation between an interior region and the larger structure within which it is visible.

NATURE develops a related account through two senses of void. The exclusive void would be absolute absence everywhere. The nonexclusive void is an irreducible background capable of containing phenomena and noumena. The fact that a reader is already perceiving a page provides the document’s example of a phenomenon incompatible with absolute absence. The nonexclusive void, by contrast, can coexist with what becomes observable, and the page speculates about a pervasive field of zero-point energy. Another example distinguishes the numerical measurement of light, such as a wavelength of 700 nanometers, from the experienced quality of redness. A partial frame of reference detects phenomena at particular places and times. The notation R(t) represents the frame at one moment, and R represents the whole succession of such states, including perception and memory. An imagined omniscient frame would have simultaneous access to all partial frames and all noumena. In the final example, an object can be directly perceived by one observer and remain noumenal to another. The journal page journal_karbytes_24december2024 records how karbytes maintained its first two onion websites on a single Lenovo laptop running Ubuntu. It calls the sites A and B and gives each a separate onion address. Its five operating practices cover how Apache distinguishes the two sites, where karbytes opens the files for B, how to inspect and restart the services involved, and how to keep Wi-Fi power saving from interrupting the connection. The closing warning concerns a different kind of reliability. A site can be reachable while some of the characters in the files it serves appear garbled. For the official versions of those files, the journal directs readers to the surface-web publications and their archived copies.

The WordPress page reproduces a plain-text journal from Karlina Object Extension Pack 27, a micro-directory within the RAW_GITHUB_FILES_MACRO_DIRECTORY of Karbytes For Life Blog. December 26 appears in the plain-text file’s header. The WordPress footer gives December 29 as the page’s last update and repeats the public-domain designation.

The first practice is to use the linked Apache configuration, 000-default.conf, to associate the two sites with different server ports. The journal delegates the exact assignments to that file. In the linked configuration, A has a virtual host on port 80, serves files from /var/www/html_a, and specifies unlisted_web_page_karbytes_first_onion_web_page.html as its directory index. B has a virtual host on port 8081, with /var/www/html_b/KARBYTES_NEW_YEAR_2025_TEST as its document root and test_darknet_website_root_directory_web_page.html as its directory index. Each virtual host has its own onion hostname. B also has separately named access and error logs. The comments date the addition of A’s directory index to October 1 and several of the virtual-host settings, including the block for B, to December 19.

The configuration records several different paths associated with B. Its document root is the KARBYTES_NEW_YEAR_2025_TEST subdirectory. Its separate directory directive applies to a path containing KARLINA_NEW_YEAR_2025_TEST and ending in KARLINA_OBJECT_extension_pack_27. The journal’s second practice concerns the broader parent directory, /var/www/html_b. It gives the command sudo nautilus /var/www/html_b to open that location in Ubuntu’s graphical file manager with elevated privileges. This is the route karbytes records for reaching and modifying the files that make up B. The Apache file specifies where requests are served from, while the Nautilus command specifies where karbytes begins browsing the files for editing.

The third practice is to inspect Tor and Apache separately. The journal gives sudo service tor status and sudo service apache2 status to see whether each service is running and how long it has been active. The two checks correspond to different parts of the arrangement. Apache serves the web content, and Tor makes the onion service reachable to visitors using Tor Browser. The status commands answer a local question about the processes on the laptop. The fourth practice uses the same two service names for a different purpose. Running sudo service tor restart and sudo service apache2 restart refreshes the services without requiring a reboot of the whole laptop. Together, the two practices record how karbytes distinguishes checking the current state from deliberately restarting the software.

The fifth practice deals with the laptop’s wireless connection. The journal identifies Wi-Fi power management as a setting that could disconnect the host while Tor and Apache are running. It calls for checking and disabling that setting whenever the server is started again. The first command, iwconfig, shows the wireless interface name and whether power management is enabled. The second, sudo iwconfig wlan0 power off, uses wlan0 as a placeholder for the actual interface name. Karbytes records wlp2s0 as the name on the Lenovo laptop at the time of writing. Substituting that name gives sudo iwconfig wlp2s0 power off for the documented machine. The sequence matters because it first establishes which interface is in use and then changes the setting for that interface. This part of the journal concerns the connection that the running services depend on, separate from the status of the services themselves.

Rule one supplies a standing qualification for the claims karbytes makes. Karbytes says it speaks without asserting absolute certainty. It intends all its propositional statements to be understood as ontological assumptions, meaning assumptions about what exists or what is the case. The qualification is implicit. Even when karbytes writes a sentence as a direct assertion, the reader is meant to understand it as expressing what karbytes assumes about reality. The hyperlink on “ontological” connects that usage to the project’s separate account of ontology. The language of the first rule already gives an instance of this approach. Karbytes names the genome and websites as central to its identity while qualifying what it claims to know about their uniqueness.

Rule two identifies the maximization of satisfaction as karbytes’ only ethical imperative. Its scope is every self-aware information-processing node throughout nature. The explanatory paragraph calls an agent of that kind a person, so the relevant category is given in terms of self-awareness and information processing. The commitment belongs to karbytes, while the satisfaction at issue belongs to each person covered by it. This allows an ethic stated from a single standpoint to concern experiences distributed across many standpoints.

The definition of satisfaction turns on a person’s judgment about the use of its own time. A person is satisfied insofar as it experiences its time as being put to good use in service of its authentic goals. The evaluation, the time being evaluated, and the goals that give the evaluation its meaning all belong to the same person. “Good” has a specific role in this definition. It describes the person’s judgment that the time it is spending serves ends that genuinely matter to it. Since the standard refers to each person’s authentic goals, the same use of time can have a different value for different people.

Dissatisfaction has an additional condition. A person wants or demands satisfaction and experiences its absence. The person concerned judges that absence to be bad. The demand matters because it identifies a situation in which satisfaction was sought and something the person wanted from its time was missing. The page connects the terms in this ethical rule to three other KARBYTES documents. Satisfaction points to the Declaration of Grand Demands, the term for the self-aware agent points to the page on agency, and the reference to authentic goals points to Karbytes Primary Values. Those links give the reader places to follow the concepts beyond the brief definitions used here.

The page also describes itself as an extension of KARBYTES_CONTENT_UPDATING_RULES, which in turn describes itself as extending this page. The companion assigns authority over revisions to the public collection to Kar Beringer and says that more than ten Earth years of inactivity can be taken as permanent closure. Here, the same person is identified as the source of the first-person statements. The companion uses that identity to say who can change KARBYTES. This journal uses it to locate the speaker, qualify the speaker’s claims about reality, and express an ethical commitment whose fulfillment is judged through each affected person’s experience of its own time.

The page karbytes_primary_values ranks four things that karbytes gives priority when deciding how to use its cognitive resources. Nature comes first, followed by consciousness, logic, and comfort. Each term answers a different question. Nature describes what reality encompasses. Consciousness describes how a portion of that reality can experience something. Logic describes the consistency that can be discerned in interactions within nature. Comfort describes how pleasure and pain figure in experience and attention. The ranking is explicitly karbytes’ own, and the numbers indicate relative priority from one through four.

The page on Karbytes For Life Blog gives September 25, 2025 as its last-update date and designates its contents as public domain. It identifies itself as a revision of a version dated June 13, 2025. That earlier version is available as a separate archived page, with its HTML source preserved in Karlina Object Extension Pack 49. I compared the current page with the archived June text, which agrees with its linked HTML source after whitespace is normalized. The June date identifies the version preserved in the archive. The archive page’s own footer also gives September 25 as its last-update date.

Nature, the highest priority, is defined as the single set encompassing all phenomena, all noumena, and two forms of pure nothingness. The page calls these non-generative nothingness and generative nothingness and relates them to zero and infinity, respectively. The linked NATURE document explains the contrast in terms of what can coexist with each form. What it now calls the exclusive void is absolute absence everywhere, extending to both phenomena and noumena. What it calls the nonexclusive void is a featureless substrate that can coexist with and permeate them. The names used in that linked document describe coexistence and leave the question of causal origin open. Zero corresponds to the case in which nothing exists or could exist. Infinity corresponds to the case in which infinitely many things exist or could exist.

The same linked document gives phenomena and noumena a meaning relative to a frame of reference. A phenomenon has distinguishable features that a frame renders and observes. Something directly observed by one frame can remain a noumenon to another frame that is situated elsewhere or has no direct access to it. The second frame can still have a thought about the thing, and that thought can itself be experienced as a phenomenon. This makes the scope of the first value much wider than the contents of karbytes’ own experience. Nature includes what any particular observer encounters, what is outside that observer’s immediate awareness, and the background in which the distinction is made.

Consciousness, ranked second, is the capacity of an allocation of nature to be aware of phenomena or noumena by rendering subjective qualia. The examples in the values page are sensory information and abstract thoughts. Its use of qualia therefore includes both what it is like to receive a sensory impression and what it is like to entertain an idea. The linked NATURE document illustrates this distinction between experience and description through color. A wavelength can be specified as a number, such as 700 nanometers, while redness is the experienced quality in the example. The measurable quantity and the subjective appearance describe different aspects of the event.

The consciousness entry also distinguishes how much an agent or frame of reference can render from what kinds of experience it can render. A difference in capacity can concern the amount of experiential content, the kinds available, or both. The term information-processing agent points to the project’s AGENCY page. That document describes an agent as a relatively finite partition of nature that renders perceptions, makes selections among possible behaviors, and carries out decisions in pursuit of goals. The values page draws on the part of that account concerned with rendering experience. Nature is the encompassing set, while a conscious agent has a particular range of experience within it.

Logic, ranked third, is the interaction of multiple components of nature in a consistent and apparently rule-based manner. The wording applies to interactions broadly and singles out abstract entities, especially symbols and quantities, as a setting in which the consistency is particularly apparent. The qualification “apparently” is part of the definition. What karbytes gives priority to is the recognizable order in how components relate to one another. The link on quantities leads to NUMBERS, where a number is defined as information representing one finite quantity. This gives a specific example of the abstract material the logic entry has in mind. A quantity can be represented by a symbol, and relations among such representations can exhibit the consistency the page calls logic.

Comfort, the fourth priority, is defined through the perceived relation of pleasure to pain. The fuller formulation involves pleasure being more prominent in experience and receiving greater priority than pain. The entry also offers a simpler formulation in which comfort consists in minimizing pain. The first formulation includes what stands out to the person and what the person gives priority to. The second allows a reduction in pain itself to count as comfort. Fourth place gives comfort the lowest rank within this particular set of priorities, while retaining it as one of the things karbytes explicitly values.

The archived June version puts the ranking in a broader frame. It describes the values as priorities for karbytes’ existence, first-person experience, and conduct. It ranks truth first, defining it as the way everything is actually configured within a singular and encompassing reality. Autonomy comes second and concerns an information-processing agent’s capacity to set and achieve its own goals. Innovation comes third and concerns changes that are relatively novel and help a system survive or help the agents using it reach their goals more effectively. Comfort comes fourth and describes a sentient agent experiencing a degree of pain it judges tolerable, especially while it also experiences pleasure. Kindness comes fifth and concerns behavior that tries to increase the comfort experienced by all sentient information-processing agents. The archived page identifies that five-value list as itself a revision of material from a June 13 journal entry.

The September version changes both the list and the purpose given for ranking it. Its introduction focuses on how karbytes allocates cognitive resources. Nature, consciousness, and logic occupy the first three positions formerly occupied by truth, autonomy, and innovation. There is continuity between the two definitions at the top. The older truth entry treats reality as a singular whole, and the newer nature entry retains that encompassing scope while naming phenomena, noumena, and forms of nothingness within it. Comfort is the only label retained in both lists. Its number stays at four, but it becomes the final entry, and its definition moves from tolerable pain in the presence of pleasure to the relative prominence and priority of pleasure or the minimization of pain. Kindness appears as a separate value in the June list. The September page presents a four-value ranking without that separate entry.

KARBYTES_RULES_FOR_ITSELF links to this page when it refers to authentic goals. In that companion, satisfaction is a person’s judgment that its time is being put to good use in service of such goals. The Primary Values page gives karbytes’ own ranking of what merits that attention. It also gives comfort a more specific role than satisfaction has in the companion. Comfort concerns the experience and priority of pleasure and pain. Satisfaction concerns whether the person regards the use of its time as serving what genuinely matters to it. In the September ranking, the first claim on karbytes’ attention is the whole of nature, followed by the capacity to experience it, the consistency discernible within it, and the qualities of experience that make it more comfortable.

The page karbytes_ontology outlines karbytes’ assumptions about what constitutes reality and how it behaves. It begins with the limits of what a frame of reference can experience, then considers how an agent pursues its goals, how pleasure and pain affect its behavior, and how recurring patterns make prediction possible. It closes by distinguishing physical laws, which it describes as assumptions about the behavior of matter and energy, from mathematical relations, which it treats as immutable. The entries give the terms specific meanings within karbytes’ framework.

The page on Karbytes For Life Blog gives September 28, 2025 as its last-update date and designates its contents as public domain. It identifies karbytes_primary_values as a companion and points to an archived September 2 version, whose HTML source is preserved in Karlina Object Extension Pack 49. I compared the two editions and checked the archived page against its linked HTML. Their text agrees after whitespace is normalized. The archived page distinguishes the September 2 version it reproduces from its own September 27 last-update date. It also identifies a still earlier version dated July 31.

The first entry defines solipsism, with the heading spelled SOLIPSIM on the page. The defining condition is that a frame of reference is aware only of what it contains. The page narrows this to the qualia available to that frame in what it experiences as its present moment. Awareness is therefore situated twice over. It belongs to a particular frame, and what is available within that frame belongs to its particular present. The definition concerns the relationship between awareness and its contents. Later entries can still refer to several agents, each with its own field of awareness.

The page uses R and R(t) to distinguish the whole history of a frame of reference from what the frame renders at one time. The variable t marks a discrete unit of time as elapsed according to R. R(t) represents the frame’s contents at the corresponding point in its timeline. The full R is described as the sum of all such instances, with the timeline providing the linear, chronological order of every quale the frame ever renders. The distinction makes room for a frame to have a history much larger than what it experiences at any one instant. A recollection available now would belong to the contents of the present state, while the original observation would occupy its own position in the history.

Selfishness extends the same account from awareness to concern and action. The page defines it as an inevitable tendency for an information-processing agent to concern itself with what transpires within its own field of awareness. It develops this idea through an agent’s ongoing pursuit of goals. The agent has a conceptual picture of its circumstances, considers possible actions, implements a decision, and receives sensory or computational feedback. The outcome matters to the agent through that feedback and through its judgment of whether the action is helping it attain its goals. Karbytes places the agent’s understanding of a situation, the decision it makes, and its assessment of the result within the same field.

The passage on altruism separates whom an action helps from the goal for which the acting agent undertakes it. In the example, agent X assists agent Y in attaining Y’s own goals. That assistance qualifies as altruistic behavior in the example because of what it does for Y. Karbytes argues that X still has to conceive and carry out the assistance primarily in service of X’s own goals. Helping Y can itself be among those goals. The claim concerns the standpoint and purpose from which X acts, even when the action enables Y to achieve something Y wants. This is how the page fits assistance to another person into its account of selfishness.

Hedonism is stated more briefly. It is the general tendency of a sentient information-processing agent to avoid experiencing pain and to seek pleasure. The two directions matter separately. An agent can act to escape or reduce an unpleasant experience, and it can act to bring about an enjoyable one. The current entry describes a tendency specific to sentient agents, whereas the preceding definition of selfishness is expressed as applying to any information-processing agent. In the framework, selfishness concerns whose field and goals organize the action. Hedonism identifies two kinds of experience that can give the action direction.

Determinism, in the September 28 edition, concerns the predictability of what an agent observes and remembers observing. An agent can predict the rendering of some qualia to a meaningful degree of accuracy because those qualia appear to be individual instances of recurring patterns. Each occurrence can be distinct while exhibiting associations that have appeared before. The consistency of those associations makes earlier observations informative about later ones. Karbytes locates the definition in the experience of the predicting agent. The agent encounters particular events, remembers some of them, and uses the regularities it can discern to anticipate what will be rendered next.

The following paragraph describes the laws of physics as assumptions about how discrete, distinct allocations of matter and energy interact and evolve. That evolution is considered as time elapses according to the observing frames of reference. The wording places physical law alongside the preceding account of prediction. A frame encounters patterns in how events unfold, and a proposed physical law describes how the material systems involved are expected to behave. The final paragraph gives mathematics a different status. It treats mathematical rules as immutable logical relations among numbers, including the relation of a number to itself. The example pairs 1 = 1 with 1 = 2 − 1. The page presents them as equivalent expressions of a fixed relation, with the right-hand side of the second expression resolving to the same value.

The archived September 2 version makes the changes in emphasis visible. Its introduction calls its contents statements about how reality works and directs readers to the Primary Values page for the priorities that constrain karbytes’ decisions. It gives hedonism a broader treatment than the September 28 version. It describes certain sufficiently complex agents as continually trying to minimize suffering and maximize pleasure. It also proposes hedonism as a basic heuristic for setting priorities. In that account, suffering can include subjective pain and damage to essential hardware or software. Pleasure can include the experience of reward, especially from reaching a goal, and the strengthening of the systems the agent depends on. The current definition concentrates on the tendency to seek pleasure and avoid pain as experiences.

The archive also gives a substantial account of compatibilism. It describes free will or agency as emerging from deterministic factors within an agent’s mental model of reality. In that model, the experience of choosing can obscure some of the unconscious and deterministic processes that underlie the behavior. The degree of agency depends on the agent’s hardware, internal software, and external environment. The archive describes a continuum whose lower endpoint is illustrated by a machine without an internal representation of itself or its environment. Its upper endpoint is a hypothetical omniscient and omnipotent being able to do anything. Between those endpoints, the agent’s sense of freedom can vary with the choices it can formulate and what it can accomplish.

To make that variation explicit, the earlier version proposes a function for the degree of agency in a particular decision. It adds the number of distinct options the agent can mentally generate before choosing one to the agent’s cumulative rate of success at attaining its goals up to that decision. One term concerns the options available at the moment of choice. The other concerns the record of what the agent has been able to achieve through its actions. The same archived passage defines determinism through the causal relationship between successive events and indeterminism through events without prior causes. The September 28 version puts the emphasis on a different aspect of determinism, the extent to which an observer can predict recurring patterns. The explicit compatibilist model and its proposed measure of agency belong to the archived edition.

The earlier mathematics entry makes a broader claim about the reach of mathematical relations. It describes them as true throughout space, time, matter, energy, and even pure nothingness. It places mathematics in a realm of pure potential independent of physical phenomena, while also giving it a role in the precise description of observed physical patterns. The archive characterizes physical laws as well-established yet open to challenge. The current version retains the immutability of mathematical relations and expresses it through a simple numerical identity, with physical laws discussed separately as assumptions about observed systems.

The companion Primary Values page gives a personal ranking of nature, consciousness, logic, and comfort. Those priorities connect to the ontology without duplicating its definitions. Nature is the encompassing reality within which frames and agents occur. Consciousness concerns the capacity to render the experience whose limits the solipsism entry describes. Logic concerns the consistency that makes patterns recognizable and underlies the final treatment of mathematics. Comfort concerns pleasure and pain as experienced and prioritized, the same two kinds of experience that the hedonism entry treats as influences on behavior. The ontology connects these ideas at the scale of an agent’s experience. An agent has a present field of awareness, acts through the goals and feedback available within that field, and can use patterns remembered from earlier experience to anticipate some of what comes next.
