BEST_PRACTICES_FOR_USING_THE_WEB is karbytes’ guide to building a personal home on the World Wide Web. It recommends making a website, regularly preserving its pages and files, and using social media to find people and share what one publishes. The three practices serve different purposes. The website gives a person a place to organize memories, interests, and knowledge. Archiving gives that work a chance to survive damage or deletion. Social platforms give it a way to reach readers and become part of conversations. The page was last updated on November 11, 2023, and designates its contents as public domain.

The first practice is to create a free site on the hosted WordPress service. Karbytes recommends the Intergalactic theme because of its streamlined layout, high-contrast colors, and sans-serif type. Navigation matters as much as appearance. In the setup the article describes, a Menu button sits in the upper-right corner of every page. Opening it reveals an alphabetical list of the site’s published pages. This gives readers a consistent way to move through a site that might contain stories, pictures, links to favorite places on the web, and pages about different interests.

Karbytes uses its two personal sites, Karlina Object and Karbytes For Life Blog, as examples. Both use Intergalactic. The article gives their author two reasons for keeping them. One is to store cherished memories in a place karbytes hopes to revisit for the rest of its life. The other is to share interests and knowledge with anyone who has unrestricted access to the surface web. The same page can therefore serve as a personal record and a public resource. The website gives those purposes a common location, with a menu through which either the author or a visitor can find the material again.

The second practice is to save each page to the Wayback Machine on a recurring basis. Karbytes describes archived copies as a way to recover pages after the live site has been altered, damaged, or deleted, whether by its author or by somebody else. A saved version can be looked up and used to reconstruct the lost page. The article expresses considerable confidence in the long-term value of these copies, while reporting several ways an individual save can fail. A capture can appear to have succeeded and later be missing. A save can also capture an earlier version when the intention was to preserve the latest one. For the latter case, the article gives a forty-five-minute wait before trying the same page again. These experiences explain why karbytes recommends making repeated saves over time. Its own stated routine was to save every page of its personal websites at least once every three months, with the intention of continuing for life.

The article reports a daily limit of ten saves for the same address and a lower limit of two for certain resources, such as raw MP4 files in public GitHub repositories. Those are the figures given in the 2023 account. Karbytes also describes what a collection of archived pages can offer when the captures are usable together. In its account, following an internal link from a saved page opens the most recent archived copy of the target. If all the site’s pages have been saved, a visitor can approximate the experience of moving through the original site using the Wayback Machine, an Internet connection, a browser, and a computer or smartphone.

A separate problem arises when a page and the media displayed inside it are captured differently. The article says a saved page can be available while embedded elements are missing. It singles out large images, playable videos, and multimedia widgets as prone to this problem. The workaround karbytes uses is to store image files, videos, and web page source files in at least one public GitHub repository. Each file can then be submitted separately using the address of its raw version. The page recommends listing those direct file references on the WordPress site as well. In that arrangement, WordPress provides a readable page and a navigable index, GitHub holds the individual artifacts, and the Wayback Machine can capture the page and the underlying files separately. The article points to KARBYTES_CODE_CONTINUES and the raw-file macro-directory in connection with that practice.

The third practice is to create and use social media accounts. Karbytes describes these services as places to follow topics of interest, take part in public or private discussions, and share ideas, images, and links. It recommends Twitter and Minds for regular use because, in the author’s experience, they allow public material to be seen by people without accounts on those services. It also mentions using LinkedIn, Patreon, Instagram, and Facebook, with Facebook used only rarely. Karbytes says its experience of Facebook is that it is more oriented toward sharing with friends and family, and it gives content censorship as another reason it prefers other services for reaching a wider audience.

Instagram receives a separate discussion. Karbytes finds it an enjoyable source of news and sees it as especially valuable to visual artists because of its emphasis on images. The article describes the profile layout in detail. Photographs and video posts appear as equal-sized clickable squares in three columns. The newest post occupies the upper-left position, and the sequence runs through to the oldest at the lower right. This gives a visitor a way to survey a body of visual work before opening individual posts. Karbytes says the home page of either personal website gives readers a way to find its profiles on Twitter, Minds, LinkedIn, Instagram, and Patreon. The closing invitation specifically names Twitter, Minds, Instagram, and LinkedIn as places to follow new material, including news articles, videos, and personal blog posts, or to respond through comments and direct messages.

Beyond setting up accounts and saving files, the article encourages readers to spend time exploring whatever interests them. It describes Wikipedia as a volunteer-built encyclopedia covering many subjects, YouTube as a collection of videos made by people ranging from ordinary contributors to experts, and Reddit as a gathering place for many topic-specific forums. The point of these examples is to describe how karbytes became comfortable using the web. Following one’s curiosity gives repeated practice finding resources, moving between sites, and discovering what kinds of participation are enjoyable.

The closing widens the time horizon. Karbytes imagines a future Internet extending from Earth to major space stations and Mars. It expects the amount of data on the web and the number of people using it to grow as long as humanity remains sufficiently large and cooperative. It also speculates that advances in medicine, such as replacing damaged tissue with tissue grown from stem cells, could someday extend lifespans indefinitely and bring the death rate close to zero. That speculation echoes the earlier aside about archiving the sites for the rest of its life. The immediate practice is more concrete. A person can start with a site worth returning to, keep copies of the pages and the files they depend on, and use the wider web to find readers, conversations, and new things to learn.

The page karbytes_04_october_2024 documents how karbytes hosted its first onion website on a Linux laptop and demonstrated it from an Android phone. It brings together three kinds of evidence. Karbytes says the project began with a search for free dark-web hosting. The services it found advertised as free were offline, and the available alternatives charged a fee. Since the intended site was a small demonstration of what an onion page could be, karbytes wanted to avoid paying a hosting provider. The Apache part of those instructions begins with refreshing the package information and installing Apache on a Debian- or Ubuntu-based system. It then opens the server’s document directory, /var/www/html, in the Nautilus file manager with administrative privileges so the custom files can be placed there. The instructions edit Apache’s 000-default.conf in the sites-available directory. The example keeps the document root at /var/www/html and sets the DirectoryIndex for the port-80 virtual host to the chosen HTML file. Restarting Apache applies the configuration. In karbytes’ setup, that change determines which page the server returns when a browser asks for the site’s root.

The Tor part installs the Tor package and edits /etc/tor/torrc. The example assigns the service directory /var/lib/tor/hidden_service/ and forwards the onion service’s port 80 to Apache on port 80 at the machine’s loopback address. After restarting Tor, the instructions read the generated onion hostname from the hostname file in that service directory. Apache and Tor have distinct roles in the arrangement. Apache supplies the page and its files. Tor supplies the route by which a Tor-capable client can request them. The journal makes remote availability conditional on the laptop being on and connected, with both services running and the correct files in place.

The October 1 photograph preserves the initial result before Apache’s default page was replaced. It shows an Android phone held horizontally against a dark background. In the journal’s sequence, this first test establishes that the phone can reach the server through the onion service while Apache is still delivering its default content.

Karbytes considered two applications for the custom page. It first tried BEATS_MACHINE, which depended on the Web Audio API, and reports encountering a restriction in its Tor browser setup. The journal includes the feature check provided by ChatGPT-4o. It looks for either AudioContext or webkitAudioContext, creates an audio context when one is available, and otherwise displays an alert explaining that sound generation will fail. Karbytes ultimately chose PI_APPROXIMATION as the interactive demonstration. The new page used a canvas and numerical readouts to make its activity visible while it ran.

The custom HTML welcomes the visitor to karbytes’ first onion page and dates its availability notice to October 1. It links back to the surface-web account of how it was created, identifies Karlina Object, Karbytes For Life Blog, and the project’s GitHub presence, and points to the separate surface-web tutorial for PI_APPROXIMATION. The linked source loads pi_approximation.js and karbytes_aesthetic.css. In the screenshot, an orange title appears on black above a pale green introduction. Cyan highlights the availability notice, yellow marks the description of the application, and green text sets out the mathematical relations. The canvas, counters, and timestamped event log occupy the lower part of the page. The counters are cyan, while the log is green, and both sit in panels outlined in magenta.

PI_APPROXIMATION is a Monte Carlo simulation. Its white canvas is a square 400 pixels on each side, with horizontal and vertical lines marking the center. The intended run lasts 3,600 seconds and plots one randomly positioned dart per second. The JavaScript generates a pair of signed integer coordinates, converts the pair to a canvas position, and tests its distance from the center. A dart at or within 200 pixels of the center is red. A dart farther away is blue. The red points therefore accumulate within the inscribed circle, while the blue points occupy the parts of the square beyond its boundary. The app divides the red count by the total count and multiplies the result by four. The reason is geometric. A circle of radius 200 occupies pi divided by four of the area of a square with side length 400. The fraction of throws landing in the circle estimates that area ratio. The displayed estimate can change with each new throw, alongside the countdown and the two color counts.

The local Firefox test comes after the change to Apache’s configuration. Karbytes says it refreshed Firefox at the laptop’s localhost address and saw that the server was now returning unlisted_web_page_karbytes_first_onion_web_page.html. The journal gives a screenshot restriction it encountered in Tor as the reason for using Firefox to record this view. The local request checks the file Apache delivers on the server machine. The photograph and video made with the Android phone document access through Tor from a separate device.

The October 2 Firefox screenshot captures a run well under way. The square contains a dense scattering of red dots through the center and blue dots concentrated toward the corners. The readout shows 2,324 seconds remaining, 1,008 red throws, 268 blue throws, and an estimate of 3.1598746081504703. The counts total 1,276, corresponding to the 1,276 seconds that have elapsed in the app’s countdown. Below them, the event log records initialization and the start of the simulation using timestamps measured in milliseconds from the Unix epoch. The still captures both the custom page Apache served and a populated state produced by its script.

Abridged Keyboard Musical Instrument

ABRIDGED_KEYBOARD_MUSICAL_INSTRUMENT is a tutorial, source-code archive, and demonstration of a browser instrument made by karbytes. The application turns selected letter and punctuation keys into musical controls. Pressing a mapped key generates a tone, turns the corresponding on-screen key green, and adds a timestamped entry to a visible console. The tutorial presents the application through three source files, a screenshot of the HTML source, two screenshots of the running interface, and a short video of karbytes playing it. It also identifies a live edition and explains how the three files can be run locally. The page’s footer gives July 12, 2025, as its last update and designates the content as public domain.

The name “abridged” describes the selection of pitches. The instrument contains twenty-two natural notes from C3 through C6, an inclusive span of three octaves. It omits the sharps and flats between those notes and the wider register of a full piano. The HTML comments describe the omission in terms of black and white piano keys and use major and minor as shorthand. The actual distinction implemented in the frequency map is between natural notes and accidentals. The available pitches can, for example, form both a C major chord and an A minor chord. The reduction makes it possible to fit the whole instrument into a compact set of ordinary computer controls.

The mapping starts on the bottom letter row of a typical keyboard. Z plays C3 at 130.81 hertz, X plays D3 at 146.83, C plays E3 at 164.81, V plays F3 at 174.61, B plays G3 at 196.00, N plays A3 at 220.00, and M plays B3 at 246.94. The comma plays middle C, or C4, at 261.63 hertz. The period plays D4 at 293.66 and the slash plays E4 at 329.63. The sequence then moves to A for F4 at 349.23 hertz, S for G4 at 392.00, D for A4 at 440.00, and F for B4 at 493.88. G plays C5 at 523.25 hertz, H plays D5 at 587.33, J plays E5 at 659.25, K plays F5 at 698.46, L plays G5 at 783.99, and the semicolon plays A5 at 880.00. Q and W finish the sequence with B5 at 987.77 hertz and C6 at 1,046.50 hertz. The screen labels identify the input controls, so the displayed A key plays F4, and the displayed C key plays E3.

The three source components have separate jobs. The HTML file, abridged_keyboard_musical_instrument.html, defines the page structure and includes styles specific to the instrument. Its header is dated September 13, 2024. The shared karbytes_aesthetic.css file supplies the general visual theme and is dated July 10, 2023. The JavaScript file, abridged_keyboard_musical_instrument.js, creates the keys and handles sound, input, and logging. Its published header is dated January 26, 2025, the date of the documented touchscreen update. The tutorial points to the twentieth KARLINA_OBJECT extension pack for the HTML and stylesheet and to the twenty-eighth for the updated script. The files are intended to sit together in one directory, with the HTML opened directly in a browser. The tones are generated by the browser, so the local instrument has no set of sound samples to download or install.

The HTML establishes an English-language document with UTF-8 encoding and a viewport declaration. The body has a title, a sentence instructing the user to play with the physical keyboard, an initially empty keyboard container, and a console container whose placeholder is three question marks. The body’s load handler calls the initialization function. The script itself creates the visual keys by walking through the frequency map in order, inserting one element for each control. Each element receives the same key class, an identifier matching its input character, and an uppercase display label where applicable. The document can therefore describe the instrument’s structure without writing out twenty-two separate pieces of keyboard markup. The same frequency map determines both the order of the visible controls and the notes the handlers play.

The interface combines a shared black-and-orange theme with instrument-specific green and gray. The stylesheet supplies the black background, orange monospace text, a fixed 800-pixel body content width, and green console lettering. It also contains general rules for input and select controls, hover colors, table cells, and a cyan output element. The HTML’s own styles center the page text and set the body padding to fifty pixels. A dark-gray panel with a two-pixel green outline frames the keyboard. The default keys are light gray, each with black lettering, an orange two-pixel outline, rounded corners, and a nominal content size of fifty by two hundred pixels. The keys are inline blocks with margins, so the continuous sequence wraps into rows. In the published screenshots, the first row has twelve keys from Z through S, and the second has ten from D through W. The second row continues the rise in pitch where the first leaves off. A separate green-bordered console below the keyboard grows vertically as messages accumulate.

The JavaScript uses the browser’s Web Audio interface, with a fallback for the WebKit-prefixed audio context name. A valid press calls the note-playing function with a frequency from the map. The function creates a fresh oscillator and gain node, selects a sine wave, sets the frequency and a gain value of 0.5, and connects the oscillator through the gain node to the audio destination. It starts the oscillator and schedules it to stop half a second later using the audio context’s clock. The resulting voice is a short synthesized tone. Each press creates its own oscillator, which allows notes triggered close together to overlap. That is how the same mechanism can produce either a melody of successive tones or several pitches at once.

The sound and the green highlight have separate lifetimes. On a physical keydown, the script reads the input character, converts letters to lowercase, looks up the corresponding pitch, calls the note-playing function, and adds the pressed class to the screen key. The keyup handler removes that class. Releasing a key early clears the highlight while the oscillator completes its scheduled half-second. Holding a key keeps the visual state active until release, and any repeated keydown events delivered by the browser can trigger additional half-second tones. The distinction matters because the source comments sometimes describe the color change in terms of half a second. In the actual handlers, the audio stop is scheduled by the note-playing function, while the color is restored by the release event.

The tutorial dates the touchscreen update to January 26, 2025, at 1 p.m. Pacific Standard Time. The new touchstart handler examines the touched element. If it is one of the displayed keys, the handler uses its identifier to find the pitch, invokes the same note-playing function, and turns the key green. The touchend handler removes the highlight. The touchscreen and physical-keyboard paths therefore share the same pitches, sound duration, and console behavior. The file karbytes_aesthetic.css is a shared stylesheet that gives karbytes’ web pages a recognizable appearance. It establishes black backgrounds, orange monospace text, cyan data displays, and green console messages. Its six rules cover the page body, form controls, hovered controls, table data cells, an element identified as output, and elements belonging to the console class. The copy in Karlina Object Extension Pack 20 is 57 lines long. Its opening comment identifies karbytes as the author, dates the stylesheet July 10, 2023, and declares it public domain.

The body rule sets the page’s baseline appearance. It gives the body a black background, represented by #000000, and an orange foreground color, #ff9000. Text uses the browser’s monospace font at sixteen pixels. Ordinary text within the body inherits that color and typography unless another rule supplies a different value. The shared styling therefore gives a page a common starting point before individual components receive their own colors or dimensions.

The body has ten pixels of padding on every side and a declared width of 800 pixels. The accompanying comment describes the intended content width as 800 pixels or less. The actual declaration sets a fixed width of 800 pixels. Under ordinary content-box sizing, the left and right padding add twenty pixels to that width, giving the body box a horizontal span of 820 pixels before any margins. A grouped rule applies the same treatment to input and select elements. Both get black backgrounds, orange text, and a solid orange border one pixel thick. A five-pixel corner radius rounds the outline, and ten pixels of padding separate the content from it. A separate grouped rule handles the hovered state of both kinds of control. When the pointer is over an input or select element, the background turns orange and the text turns black. The two declarations reverse the colors of the resting state. Since the stylesheet already uses those two colors for the ordinary control, the reversal gives an immediate visual indication of hover within the same palette.

The table rule addresses individual data cells through the td selector. It colors the text cyan, #00ffff, and gives each cell a solid cyan border one pixel thick. Ten pixels of padding on all four sides set the cell’s contents away from the border. This creates a consistent treatment for values displayed in a table. The same cyan appears in the rule for the identifier output. That selector changes the text color of the element identified as output, allowing a page to give a result the same visual emphasis as table data even when the result appears elsewhere. Its use of an identifier is more specific than the body rule from which ordinary text inherits its orange color. The output rule is also narrower in what it sets. The final rule applies to elements with the class console. It sets bright green text, #00ff00, against a black background. The file abridged_keyboard_musical_instrument.js supplies the behavior of karbytes’ browser-based keyboard. It defines 22 pitches, creates a corresponding set of on-screen keys, turns input events into synthesized notes, and records each note trigger in a timestamped console. The file matches the JavaScript linked by the WordPress tutorial. Compared with the September 2024 version in Extension Pack 20, the executable additions are the two touchscreen listeners.

The script works with the instrument’s HTML and CSS through two elements. The element identified as keyboard receives the keys the script builds. The element identified as console receives the initialization and note messages. The script also creates a shared audio context when it is evaluated, choosing the standard AudioContext constructor when available and otherwise using webkitAudioContext. That context supplies the clock and output destination for all the notes played during the session. The browser generates the sound from the frequencies specified in the program.

The pitch map, named noteFrequencies, is a lookup object with a constant binding. Its keys are the characters reported by the physical computer keyboard or stored as identifiers on the virtual keys. Its values are frequencies in hertz. Comments beside the values give the corresponding musical note names. The entries are ordered from lowest to highest and use the natural-note sequence across three octaves, starting with C3 and ending with C6. The inclusive endpoints give the instrument 22 distinct pitches.

The lowest seven inputs are Z, X, C, V, B, N, and M. They play C3 at 130.81 hertz, D3 at 146.83, E3 at 164.81, F3 at 174.61, G3 at 196.00, A3 at 220.00, and B3 at 246.94. A, S, D, and F continue with F4 at 349.23, G4 at 392.00, A4 at 440.00, and B4 at 493.88.

When the player selects a key, the code retrieves its assigned pitch and passes that value to the sound generator.

The same dictionary determines the visible order of the keys. The function createKeyUI receives one character, creates a DIV, gives it the class key, and assigns the original character as its identifier. Its text content is the uppercase version of the label, so letter keys appear as capitals and punctuation retains its familiar form. The element is then appended to the keyboard container. The script applies that function to every entry in the dictionary as it is evaluated. The keys therefore appear in ascending pitch order, with the arrangement into rows handled by the HTML’s styling. Their identifiers also give the input handlers a way to find or recognize the exact elements to highlight.

Two helpers prepare messages for the event log. The function generate_time_stamp calls Date.now and returns text expressing the result as milliseconds since midnight at the start of January 1, 1970, the Unix epoch. This is a wall-clock timestamp. The audio context’s current time is used separately for scheduling sound. The function generate_paragraph_html_element wraps a supplied value in opening and closing paragraph tags. It checks that the value has a numeric length property and logs an exception if the operation fails. The source comments explain that the paragraph tags are assembled from individual character strings so the WordPress editor can display the code without treating those fragments as page markup.

The initialization function is called by the companion HTML when the page loads. It checks the audio-context variable and attempts the same construction if a context is needed. It then builds a message naming initialize_application and giving the time at which it was called. The message goes to the browser’s developer console, and a paragraph containing it replaces the contents of the visible console element. This is why a newly loaded instrument shows an initialization entry before any notes have been played. The script has already created the key elements from the dictionary. Initialization establishes the first entry in the log beneath them.

The sound routine, playNote, takes a frequency as its argument. For each call it creates a new oscillator and a gain node. It selects a sine waveform, assigns the requested frequency at the audio context’s current time, and sets the gain value to 0.5. The oscillator is connected to the gain node, and the gain node is connected to the context’s destination. The oscillator starts immediately and is scheduled to stop at the current audio time plus half a second. Since each call has its own oscillator, a second note can begin while a previous one is still within its scheduled duration. Closely timed inputs can therefore produce overlapping tones.

After scheduling the sound, playNote makes a message that names the function, includes the supplied frequency, and records when it was called. It prints the message to the browser’s developer console and appends its paragraph to the visible console. Initialization replaces the panel’s contents, while playing adds to them. As a result, a session leaves a running record of the notes triggered since initialization. Both routines retrieve the console element by its identifier and assign it to console_div, an implicit shared variable in the companion page’s classic-script context. The paragraph helper, initialization routine, key creation routine, and sound routine each catch exceptions and report them through the browser console.

The physical keyboard uses two listeners registered on the document. On keydown, the script converts the event’s key value to lowercase and checks it against the pitch map. A matching value triggers playNote. The handler then finds the virtual key with that identifier and adds the pressed class. The companion HTML styles that class in bright green. On keyup, the script finds the corresponding element and removes the class, returning it to the ordinary gray appearance. The note and the highlight thus have separate lifetimes. The oscillator’s stop time is set when the note starts, and the class is removed when the release arrives. A repeated keydown delivered by the browser goes through the same lookup and sound routine as the first press.

The two touchscreen listeners use the same elements and functions. On touchstart, the handler examines the event target. If it carries the key class, the script takes its identifier, converts it to lowercase, and looks up the assigned frequency. A match calls playNote and adds the pressed class to that target. On touchend, the handler again checks for the key class and removes the pressed state. The touch path therefore reaches the same oscillator, gain setting, half-second schedule, and timestamped note message as the physical key path. The difference is where the lookup character comes from. Physical input supplies the character through the keyboard event. Touch supplies it through the identifier of the element contacted on screen.

The January revision extends input while reusing the instrument’s existing representation of a note. A single dictionary entry provides the displayed label, the identifier used by the handlers, and the frequency given to the oscillator. When an input arrives, that entry produces an audible event, a temporary visual state, and a console entry that can still be read after the sound and highlight have ended.

sound_track_looper_two.js is the JavaScript that drives SOUND_TRACK_LOOPER_TWO, a browser application that plays selected MP3 recordings on repeat and shows how far each has progressed through the session. The source header credits karbytes, dates this version to January 26, 2025, and marks it as public domain. The companion HTML calls the script when the page loads, when the user adds a selection menu, and when the user starts playback. The script fills those menus, creates an audio player and two counters for every selection, and records the times of the three kinds of user-interface action.

The script begins with a global variable named track_count, initialized to one. That value accounts for the selector already present in the HTML, sound_file_menu_0. It counts menu slots, with each slot representing one potential playback instance. On load, load_web_page() creates a timestamped message and looks for that first selector. If it finds the element, it fills it with the options returned by get_menu_options() and reports successful population in the browser’s developer console. If the element is missing, it reports that condition there. It then writes the page-load message into console_display, the visible log at the bottom of the application.

get_menu_options() assembles a string of eleven HTML option elements. This archived version uses a common media directory on a Tor onion service. The function joins that directory to each filename to produce the option’s value, which is the media address the player will use. The visible label is the filename itself. The first option receives the selected attribute, so every newly populated menu initially shows the April 2022 frog recording. The filenames appear in the order of the array in the source. The eleven choices include two wildlife recordings, three drum recordings, and six guitar recordings.

The wildlife entries are frogs_croaking_in_castro_valley_california_21_april_2022.mp3 and coyote_vocalizations_01_july_2023.mp3. It creates a paragraph and a new selector, gives the selector the identifier sound_file_menu_ followed by the current value of track_count, and fills it with the same eleven options. The first click therefore creates sound_file_menu_1, the second creates sound_file_menu_2, and the numbering continues from there. The function puts the selector inside the paragraph, appends that paragraph to the menu area, increments track_count, and adds a timestamped entry to the visible log. Because each selector holds its own choice, two menus can point to the same recording and still produce two separately tracked playback instances.

Two helpers connect the selected menu item to the playback display. get_selected_menu_option_value() takes a selector’s identifier, finds the corresponding element, reads its selectedIndex, and returns the value of the option at that index. This gives the start routine the media address associated with the choice. extractSoundFileName() takes that address and returns the substring after its final slash. The program uses that shorter string as the track name in the interface. If filename extraction encounters an exception, the helper reports it to the developer console and returns an empty string. An exception during the selected-option lookup is also reported to the developer console, and that function falls through with an undefined result.

The start_sound_track_looper() function begins by capturing the time of the Start click and creating a local array named sound_track_array. It finds the output panel, the visible log, and the Add and Start buttons. It hides both buttons by setting their display styles to none and clears the output panel’s initial message. Then it visits the selectors in numerical order, beginning at zero and continuing through the last slot counted by track_count. For each slot, it extracts the selected filename for display and passes the full media address to a new Audio object. It stores a record containing the filename, the audio object, a loops_completed value of zero, and an elapsed_seconds value of zero. These records represent the choices captured when the session starts.

For each record, the script adds a group of paragraphs to the output panel. A line of three spaced asterisks introduces the group, followed by lines labeled Sound Track, Loops Completed, and Seconds Elapsed. Another separator follows the last group. The two numbers live inside separately addressable spans. The first track uses loops_completed_0 and elapsed_seconds_0, the next uses loops_completed_1 and elapsed_seconds_1, and each additional track gets its own pair. The identifiers are based on menu position, so two instances of the same filename can have distinct numbers on the page. Once a group is in place, the script calls play() for that audio object and registers its timer. All of the initial playback requests occur within the same Start action, allowing the recordings to play concurrently as their media becomes ready.

Every track gets a timer scheduled at one-second intervals. Each callback has access to its own index in sound_track_array and to the identifiers of its two display spans. The callback first adds one to elapsed_seconds and writes the new number to the elapsed display. It then checks the audio object’s ended property. If that property is true, the callback adds one to loops_completed, updates the loop display, and calls play() again on the same audio object. The initial playback request starts with zero completed loops. A count is credited when a timer callback observes the end of a pass, just before the script requests another pass.

This timing model lets tracks accumulate different loop totals even when their elapsed displays match. Each track’s timer starts during setup, so its displayed seconds can include time spent waiting for media to load. If a callback is delayed, it adds one when it finally runs. The displayed seconds are therefore a count of callbacks scheduled one second apart. The completion check also happens on those callbacks, so a track can reach its end between checks and wait for the next one before the script requests a restart. Different recording durations and readiness times can make the layers start and repeat at different moments. The timers and audio objects belong to the current page session, which the user can end by refreshing or closing the page.

The green action log uses a separate clock. generate_time_stamp() reads Date.now() and formats the result as milliseconds since midnight UTC on January 1, 1970. The load routine uses this timestamp to replace the initial contents of console_display. The Add routine captures its timestamp at entry and appends it after inserting the new menu. The Start routine captures its timestamp before setting up the players and appends the message after it has gone through the selected slots. That entry dates the click that initiated playback. The per-track figures in the output panel then follow the timer callbacks and the audio objects’ reported ends.

The source retains an older load_web_page() implementation inside a block comment. That version refers to fixed elements for elapsed seconds, completed loops, and the selected file. It also explicitly makes the Start button visible. In the executable implementation, the load handler fills the first selector and writes the log entry. The numbered playback displays are created later, inside start_sound_track_looper(), after the user has determined how many tracks to play. The commented code preserves the earlier layout assumptions alongside the current approach of building one display per selection.

The main routines use try-and-catch blocks to report exceptions in the developer console. The start routine hides the controls before constructing the players, so a synchronous setup exception after that point can leave some of the new interface in place until a refresh. The play() calls are issued without awaiting their results, and the timer callbacks run after the start routine has returned. As a result, a track’s elapsed display can advance while its first playback request is still pending. The visible Start timestamp and the live counters describe different stages of that process.

I checked the raw script in a local simulation with two selectors and simulated audio objects. Both selectors received eleven options, and I used the default frog recording for one and the August 20, 2024 guitar recording for the other. After two simulated timer ticks, both elapsed counters showed two. When I marked the frog audio object as ended, its loop count advanced to one and it received a second playback request. The guitar count stayed at zero with its initial playback request. That exercise verified how the script routes each timer update and replay request to the corresponding track.

GEOMETRIC_KALEIDOSCOPE_MORPH is a tutorial by karbytes for a single-page browser animation made from colored squares and equilateral triangles. The shapes travel in circular orbits, spin about their own centers, and appear in rotated and reflected copies around the center of a black canvas. Their changing overlaps give the animation its kaleidoscopic appearance. The tutorial introduces the effect, links to the development conversation, provides the complete source in a scrollable code box, and shows a screenshot of one randomly generated frame. The page and the source file are dated July 21, 2025, and marked as public domain.

The tutorial lists one application component, geometric_kaleidoscope_morph.html, in KARLINA_OBJECT Extension Pack 46. That file contains the HTML, CSS, and JavaScript needed to run the animation. The instructions tell the reader to save a copy under that filename and open it in a browser such as Firefox. They also explain how WordPress displays source code. The page escapes the angle brackets in its markup so that a reader sees the HTML tags as text inside the code box. Long lines can be read by scrolling horizontally. I compared the printed code with the linked HTML, and the source text agrees.

The running application has a different presentation from the tutorial page. The browser tab is titled Geometric Kaleidoscope Morph, and the page itself is occupied by a single canvas. The CSS removes the default margins and padding, suppresses scrollbars, and gives the viewport a black background. The script sizes the canvas to the window’s inner width and height and obtains its two-dimensional drawing context. An initial resize call establishes those dimensions before the shapes are generated. A window resize then changes the drawing surface, and the next frame applies the existing shape offsets to the new center. Opening the document afresh generates another randomized arrangement.

At startup, the script creates sixty base shapes. Each has a side count of three or four, chosen with equal probability. Each also has a size, a color, an opacity, a base point for its orbit, a radius for that orbit, an initial orbital phase, and an angular speed. The base coordinates are sampled from zero to half the initial viewport width and height. Once the canvas origin is translated to its center, those positive coordinates place the base points of the unrotated, unreflected copy in the lower-right quarter. The circular paths can carry the moving shapes across those quarter boundaries. The other transformations distribute copies of the same arrangement around the center.

The source uses two different radii. A shape’s size is the distance from its center to a vertex, chosen from twenty to just under sixty pixels. Its orbital radius is the distance from the shape’s moving center to the fixed point around which it travels, chosen from twenty to just under 120 pixels. The starting phase can lie anywhere around the orbit. Each shape’s angular speed is chosen from 0.001 to just under 0.003 radians per millisecond. That gives a full circuit a period of roughly 2.1 to 6.3 seconds. Individual shapes consequently pass through different phases and overtake one another in the changing arrangement.

Color comes from a seven-color palette of cyan, magenta, yellow, green, red, indigo, and orange. For each shape, the script chooses a palette color and adjusts its red, green, and blue channels independently. The adjustment is an integer from minus twenty through plus nineteen, and the resulting channel is kept within the valid range from zero to 255. An alpha value from 0.5 to just under 1.0 is then added. The result is a shade that can range from distinctly translucent to almost opaque. Wherever one polygon crosses another, some of the color beneath it can show through. Intersections form tinted patches with their own straight edges, so the apparent shapes in a crowded region can be more intricate than the original squares and triangles.

For each frame, the script uses the browser’s animation timestamp to calculate a motion angle for each shape. Multiplying the timestamp by the shape’s speed gives the angle used for its spin. Adding the initial phase gives the angle used for its orbit. The cosine and sine of that orbital angle locate the polygon’s center at a fixed distance from its base point. The polygon is then drawn by placing its vertices at equal angular intervals around the moving center. The triangle’s vertices are separated by 120 degrees and the square’s by 90 degrees. The script joins them into a closed path and fills it with the assigned color. Each polygon is drawn from its initial side count and color throughout the session. The visible morph comes from changing positions, orientations, and overlaps across the pattern.

The kaleidoscope effect comes from a second level of rotation, applied to the whole collection. At the start of a frame, the script clears the canvas and moves the drawing origin to the center of the window. It then makes sixteen drawing passes, turning the coordinate system by another 22.5 degrees for each successive placement. On every odd-numbered pass, it also flips the local vertical coordinate. This gives eight unreflected placements spaced 45 degrees apart and eight reflected placements interleaved between them. The same sixty shapes are drawn in every pass at the same animation time. I checked the drawing calls with a stand-in canvas and confirmed 960 polygon fills, sixteen rotation calls, and eight vertical reflections for one frame.

The source calls these placements sectors. Each pass draws the complete set of polygons, so adjacent placements can overlap across the angles that separate them. The resulting geometry repeats in eight orientations with mirror relationships between them. The order of the translucent fills and the rectangular edge of the viewport also affect the final image. The tutorial’s opening describes the shapes as spinning counterclockwise. In the code, the increasing angle moves clockwise on the unreflected canvas, where the vertical coordinate increases downward. The reflected placements reverse that direction. The result contains both senses of spin and orbital motion within the same frame sequence.

After all sixteen passes, the script restores the drawing context and requests another frame from the browser. The next callback uses its new timestamp to compute the current positions and redraw the pattern. Clearing the canvas at the beginning of each frame exposes the black background again, so the color layering comes from polygons overlapping in that frame. The animation continues across each shape’s individual orbital cycles. Because the shapes have independently chosen speeds and phases, a completed orbit for one of them can bring it back into a different arrangement of neighbors.

The tutorial’s screenshot is a wide image measuring 1300 by 651 pixels. I checked that the embedded copy and the separately archived image show the same pixels. The frame has a roughly circular black opening at the center, with a scalloped edge made by the inward-facing tips and corners of nearby polygons. A dense band of red, orange, dark purple, and wine-colored overlaps surrounds it. Farther out, bright yellow triangles and magenta shapes make a conspicuous ring, with small green triangles set among them. The eye can follow the recurrence of these color groups around the center even where individual polygons cross and partly obscure one another.

Toward the left and right edges, the pattern breaks into larger-looking clusters of cyan, bright green, orange, and violet. Some triangles and squares are easy to trace from edge to edge. Others show through several layers, producing muted blues, reddish browns, or darker purples where the fills meet. Black gaps separate some of the outer clusters, and fragments of polygons are cut off at all four edges of the picture. The central opening and the surrounding rings are features of this particular frame. They arise from where the repeated polygons happen to lie and how much of the black background they leave exposed.

As the visible area shrinks, neighboring pixels represent closer and closer points in the complex plane. If two such points round to the same representable coordinate, recalculating both pixels can produce the same result. The reply identifies JavaScript’s double-precision Number representation as the constraint it suspects. It suggests slowing the per-frame multiplier to between 1.001 and just under 1.002 so karbytes can observe when the problem appears. The magnifications mentioned in the exchange are diagnostic examples. The relevant numerical spacing depends on the scale of the view and the coordinates around its center.

ChatGPT offers three possible approaches. One is to use arbitrary-precision arithmetic through a library such as decimal.js or big.js. That would require carrying the higher-precision representation through the center coordinates, zoom, pixel mapping, interpolation, and complex iteration. A second is to move the computation to a GPU shader, with separate techniques such as split floating-point values proposed for extending usable precision. The third is to periodically recenter and rescale the coordinate space, keeping the numerical zoom factor within a more manageable range. The proposed rebasing patch checks whether the zoom has passed 100,000 after applying the normal zoom multiplier. ChatGPT describes three ways to construct that illusion. The first is to tile a two-dimensional texture in logarithmic space. A pattern such as noise or a spiral could wrap by modular arithmetic, so a chosen increase in magnification, such as eightfold or sixteenfold, returns to the same arrangement. The second is to remap a radial pattern or noise texture into polar coordinates. The angle wraps around the center, and the radial part can be designed to repeat after a chosen change of scale. The third is to place a scaled and rotated copy of a base texture inside itself, then repeat that operation for several levels. As the view moves inward, it encounters another version of the original arrangement. The inner copy can provide a visually matching point at which to restart the camera.

geometric_kaleidoscope_morph.html is a self-contained browser animation by karbytes. Its source header is dated July 21, 2025, and marks the file as public domain. The document declares HTML5, English, and UTF-8, and sets the browser tab title to Geometric Kaleidoscope Morph. The body contains a canvas and the script that draws on it. All the styling, random generation, geometry, and animation scheduling are bundled in the file. When opened as an HTML document, it begins drawing automatically.

The stylesheet makes the canvas occupy an uninterrupted black viewing area. It removes margins and padding from the document, suppresses scrollbars, and displays the canvas as a block. The script retrieves the canvas by its identifier and obtains its two-dimensional drawing context. A function named resize sets the canvas’s pixel dimensions to the browser window’s inner width and height, storing those dimensions for later calculations. The script calls resize before generating any shapes and also registers it for subsequent window resizes. Shape generation occurs outside the resize function. After a resize, the next frame uses the new center of the drawing surface and the original shape offsets measured in pixels. Reloading the document generates a new collection.

Two constants determine the amount of geometry. SHAPE_COUNT is sixty, the number of independently generated shape objects. NUM_SECTORS is sixteen, the number of times that collection is placed around the canvas center in each frame. The program fills its shapes array at startup by calling createShape sixty times. Each object stores a base point, an orbital phase, a size, an angular speed, an orbital radius, a color, and a vertex count. The same stored properties supply the source geometry for each frame and for each of its transformed placements.

The base point is specified by baseX and baseY. Each coordinate is chosen between zero and half the corresponding initial viewport dimension. Once the drawing origin is translated to the center, these coordinates put the untransformed base points in the lower-right quarter of the view. Each point acts as the anchor around which a polygon travels. The orbit begins at an angle chosen anywhere within a full turn and has a radius between twenty and just under 120 pixels. The shape also has a separate radius from its own center to each vertex, between twenty and just under sixty pixels. The two radii control different aspects of the drawing. One determines how far the polygon travels from its anchor, and the other determines the size of the polygon itself. The vertex count is independently selected as three or four with equal probability, yielding an equilateral triangle or a square.

The color generator starts with seven hexadecimal colors. They are cyan, magenta, yellow, green, red, indigo, and orange. For each shape, the script chooses one of those colors and passes it to randomShade. That function removes the initial hash sign, reads the six digits as a hexadecimal integer, and extracts the red, green, and blue bytes. It independently adds an integer from minus twenty through plus nineteen to each channel and clamps the result to the range from zero to 255. It then adds an alpha value from about one-half to one and returns a color string for the canvas. This produces variations within each color family and gives the fills different degrees of translucency. Where polygons overlap, later fills can partly reveal colors underneath, creating visible subdivisions and mixed shades within the larger shapes.

The function drawShape computes the position and orientation of one polygon at a given frame timestamp. It multiplies the timestamp, in milliseconds, by the shape’s assigned angular speed to obtain an angle. The speed is chosen from approximately 0.001 to 0.003 radians per millisecond, giving a full turn of roughly 2.1 to 6.3 seconds. The program adds the shape’s initial phase to that angle to determine the center’s position on its circular orbit. The cosine supplies the horizontal displacement from the base point, and the sine supplies the vertical displacement. Both are multiplied by the orbital radius. The original motion angle, before the phase is added, determines the polygon’s orientation around its own moving center.

The spin and the orbit therefore share one angular speed for each shape. The center-to-vertex displacement and the base-to-center displacement turn together, with a fixed angular relationship. Their sum is the displacement from the base point to the vertex. This means the same motion can be understood as a rigid polygon turning about its assigned base point. The center travels around that point, and every vertex follows its own circle around it. Different shapes have different speeds and starting phases, so their relative positions and overlaps keep changing.

Once the moving center and orientation have been calculated, drawRegularPolygon builds the outline. It begins a path and places the vertices at equally spaced angles on a circle around that center. The three vertices of a triangle are separated by 120 degrees, and the four vertices of a square are separated by 90 degrees. The first vertex starts the path, the subsequent vertices extend it with straight lines, and the final edge closes it. The canvas fills the resulting path with the shade set by drawShape. Each new frame draws the polygons using their original vertex counts. The morph in the title describes the changing arrangement made by their motion and overlap.

The render function creates the kaleidoscope effect by drawing the entire collection under sixteen transforms. It clears the canvas, saves the drawing context, and translates the origin to the current midpoint of the window. The first pass draws with an angle of zero. Each successive pass increases the angle by one-sixteenth of a full turn, or 22.5 degrees. On odd-numbered passes, the program also multiplies the local vertical coordinate by minus one, reflecting the geometry across the local horizontal axis before placing the copy at its assigned angle. A save and restore around each pass gives the next pass a fresh transform based on the centered origin.

The sixteen placements consist of eight unreflected copies spaced 45 degrees apart and eight reflected copies interleaved between them. As geometric transforms, they give the arrangement eightfold rotational repetition and eight mirror axes. Combining a rotation with the local reflection produces a mirror axis at half that rotation angle. The first such axis is 11.25 degrees from the horizontal, and the others are spaced 22.5 degrees apart. The code calls the drawing passes sectors, and each pass draws the full collection across the canvas. A polygon can cross the space between two sector directions and overlap polygons from other passes. The rectangular viewport clips some of the outer geometry, and the order of the translucent fills affects the colors where the copies overlap.

The comments describe counterclockwise spin as a premise. In the implemented coordinates, the unreflected polygons follow increasing sine and cosine angles on a display whose vertical axis points downward. Viewed on screen, their spins and orbits run clockwise. The odd-numbered passes reverse the direction through reflection. The two directions appear together, with each reflected copy following the motion of its corresponding unreflected shape in mirror form.

After drawing all sixteen passes, the program restores the original drawing context and calls requestAnimationFrame to schedule the next frame. The script makes its first such request at startup. Each callback receives a new browser timestamp and calculates the positions directly from it, so the prescribed motion depends on elapsed time. Clearing the canvas exposes the black background again, and the overlaps are composed anew from the polygons in the current frame. The individual orbits recur on their own schedules. As they come back around, they meet other shapes at different phases, producing new clusters, intersections, and openings in the pattern.

I traced the exact script at two timestamps using a stand-in drawing context. Each sample frame issued sixteen rotation calls, eight vertical reflections, and 960 polygon fills. The trace also confirmed that a sampled vertex followed the rigid rotation predicted from its base point and angular speed. After a simulated resize, the script drew that same local geometry from the new viewport center. Those checks show how the animation combines a small collection of persistent shape parameters with a changing timestamp and a repeated set of spatial transforms.

falling_apples_game_two_logic.js is the JavaScript that runs Falling Apples Game Two. It starts a round when the page loads, generates falling apples, moves the basket in response to input, scores catches, updates the elapsed-time display, and handles the end of play. It also fits the displayed playfield to the browser viewport. The source header credits karbytes, dates the file to August 17, 2025, and marks it as public domain.

The script obtains the element named game-canvas and its two-dimensional drawing context. The companion HTML defines a logical canvas 400 pixels wide and 600 pixels high. The basket is a rectangle sixty pixels wide and twenty pixels tall. Its vertical position is computed once from the canvas height, placing its top at coordinate 570 and its bottom ten pixels above the bottom of the canvas. Its horizontal position is stored separately and changes as the player moves. The main game state also includes a list of active apples, a count of apples spawned, a limit of one hundred apples, the current score, an elapsed-seconds counter, and a flag indicating whether the round is running.

The resetGame function establishes the starting state for both the first round and later rounds. It empties the apple list, places the basket at horizontal coordinate 170 so it is centered, and zeros the spawn count, score, and elapsed time. It writes the zero values into the score and timer elements and hides the Restart button. It focuses the canvas if the focus method is available and calls the game loop to draw the opening state.

A separate interval calls spawnApple every 500 milliseconds. The spawner adds an apple when the round is active and the count is below one hundred. Each apple starts at vertical coordinate zero. Its horizontal coordinate is obtained by multiplying a random fraction by the canvas width minus twenty. On the 400-pixel field, that puts the stored coordinate between zero and just under 380, leaving room for a twenty-pixel-wide circle. The spawner stores each apple as an object with x and y coordinates, appends it to the active list, and increases the count of generated apples. The half-second interval is installed once for the page and serves each round, with the game flag and count governing when it produces apples.

The basket moves in twenty-pixel steps. Clicking the left button subtracts twenty from its horizontal position, and clicking the right button adds twenty. The left handler clamps the result at zero. The right handler clamps it at the canvas width minus the basket width, which is 340 in the supplied interface. The whole basket therefore fits between the two edges even if the player keeps issuing commands in the same direction. The document-level keyboard handler recognizes the left and right arrows as well as A and D, with letter case ignored. Those inputs make the same twenty-pixel moves and suppress their default browser action. The separate scroll-key guard also covers the up and down arrows and the event names used for the spacebar. Each animation callback updates the apples before drawing the scene. The updateApples function walks backward through the active list, increasing each apple’s y-coordinate by one logical pixel. The backward traversal lets it remove an apple without skipping the entries it still has to examine. The draw function then clears the entire canvas and draws the basket before the remaining apples. The basket is a filled brown rectangle in the color #795548. Each apple is a filled red circle with a radius of ten pixels. Its center is drawn ten pixels to the right and ten pixels below its stored coordinates, so those stored values describe the top-left corner of the circle’s bounding square. Clearing the canvas exposes the pale background supplied by the companion stylesheet.

The catch test uses the apple’s bottom edge and horizontal center. The bottom edge is its y-coordinate plus twenty. It becomes eligible when that value reaches the basket’s top. At the same time, the apple’s horizontal center, its x-coordinate plus ten, must fall on or between the basket’s left and right edges. Both horizontal endpoints are included. On the supplied canvas, the first eligible vertical position is y equals 550, where the bottom of the apple reaches coordinate 570. From then on, the apple can qualify if the basket moves beneath its center before the update loop removes it. The test is a vertical threshold combined with horizontal center alignment.

The order of the checks affects the final moment at which an apple can score. The code evaluates the catch condition first. On a frame where the catch condition fails, the apple is removed as a miss once its stored top passes the canvas height. With one-pixel updates on the 600-pixel canvas, that happens at y equals 601. An apple aligned with the basket on that same update can satisfy the catch test before the miss condition is reached. For a successful catch, the function requests the sound, removes the apple, increments the score, and writes the new total to the scoreboard. A miss removes the apple and leaves the score at its previous value.

The sound helper, play_sound_file, creates a new browser Audio object for alert_sound_effect.wav and calls play. The filename is relative to the page, and the source comment describes the file as about a second long and reminiscent of coins clinking. Each catch makes its own playback request. The update routine proceeds with removing the apple and awarding the point after the request.

The falling motion, spawn interval, and elapsed-time display use three schedules. The animation adds one pixel of descent per frame callback. The spawn interval aims to introduce an apple every half second, and the elapsed interval adds one to the timer display every second. At sixty frame callbacks per second, an apple takes about 9.2 seconds of updates to reach the first catch position and about ten seconds to reach the miss threshold. The hundredth spawn occurs around fifty seconds into an uninterrupted initial round. The round continues while apples are still active, which accounts for the remaining time after the final spawn. The gameLoop function begins by checking the running flag, then updates and draws the scene. ## First-person reconstruction

I imagine the first tick as a pinpoint, almost like the edge of a thin metal disc touching glass. The higher answer arrives so close behind that I would group the two pitches into one little upward gesture. There is something of a coin clink in the brightness and the quick release. The precise spacing of the echoes makes me picture that physical texture being used as the vocabulary of an interface. The effect seems to say *registered* in the time it takes a finger to lift from a button.

I find the stereo tail particularly expressive. The opening feels small and definite. Then a version of it seems to step out to the left, and another trace appears on the right. I picture a glint moving across a surface, with the source of the glint already gone by the time the last reflection reaches me. The late right-channel ring would be easy to overlook in the game. In isolation, it gives the cue a sense of depth that its short duration might lead me to underestimate.

Placed against the game’s simple red circles and brown basket, I would take the sound as the moment the catch becomes certain. Its high register gives a tiny object an outsized sparkle. I can imagine a quick series of catches turning the repeated cue into an irregular miniature rhythm, with one bright attack arriving while the previous one is still trailing off to the side. The point of emphasis stays at the beginning of each cue, where the game registers the action.

The final silence changes how I imagine the whole second. The gesture is over early, and the rest of the file gives it room to settle. In a quiet setting I would notice the last thin ring and then the empty space after it. In a busy round, my attention would already be back on the next apple. That brief shift from the sound back to the playfield is part of what makes the cue feel suited to the action.

Falling Apples Game Two is a browser game by karbytes in which the player moves a basket across the bottom of a field to catch falling apples. A round releases one hundred apples, and each catch earns one point. The score appears out of one hundred alongside the elapsed time in seconds. The round starts as soon as the page loads. Red circles begin entering from the top, the timer advances, and the basket starts in the middle of the field.

The deployed interface is centered on a black background. A red apple emoji precedes the bright green monospace title, and the score and time occupy a single line immediately below it. The field is a tall, pale cyan rectangle with a two-pixel green border. The basket is a short, solid brown bar with a small gap between it and the bottom edge. Two pale buttons beneath the field display left and right arrows. The Restart button occupies that same control row after a round ends. In the opening view I examined, the focused canvas had a pale outline. The green border was clear after focus moved to an arrow button.

The canvas’s logical drawing surface is 400 by 600 pixels. The distinction lets the same game geometry appear at different sizes on screen. Each animation callback moves it down one logical pixel. The next frame clears the canvas and redraws the basket and every apple still in play. At sixty callbacks per second, an apple takes about 9.2 seconds of updates to reach the first catch position and about ten seconds to pass below the field. The end condition is the completion of all one hundred drops, with each apple resolved as a catch or a miss.

STEP 0. PUBLIC_DOMAIN

PUBLIC_DOMAIN sets out karbytes’ publication policy for the source code and media that make up Karlina Object and Karbytes For Life Blog. Karbytes, also identified on the page as Kar Beringer, describes those files as public-domain intellectual property. Under the page’s definition, anyone may access, possess, modify, and distribute copies. The page develops that policy by defining a digital file, explaining what changes when a file is copied, and considering how different people might claim control over copies with identical contents. It gives October 1, 2025, as its last update date and applies its public-domain designation to the page’s own contents.

The opening illustration shows three nested yellow-green box forms drawn in perspective. The smaller forms sit progressively down and to the left within the larger ones. Heavy black lines delineate the planes, deep black areas suggest recesses, and the shaded left faces give the boxes volume against a pale, lightly mottled background. The drawing makes a single form recognizable across several scales. The text that follows considers a different kind of repetition, in which an exact digital copy can preserve every bit of its source.

The page defines a file as a named, finite, ordered sequence of binary digits that encodes information. Digital electronic computers can store that sequence and transmit it to one another. An exact copy preserves the sequence even when it resides on a different physical substrate. The page argues that this process can continue indefinitely without loss of the original informational content, provided the hardware used to copy and store each successive instance functions correctly. The sequence is finite, while the number of times it can be reproduced is potentially unlimited.

Within the collection, the page divides files into code and media. A code file expresses a complete, coherent set of instructions in a programming language a computer can understand and execute. The example is factorial.cpp. Media is the broader category covering files outside that definition, including plain text, images, audio, and video. Its example is the orange-box illustration used as the Karlina Object WordPress logo. Both examples come from the Summer 2023 Starter Pack. The page then defines intellectual property as a collection containing a natural number of files. It uses property in the general sense of something a person can own and control.

OPEN_SOURCE has a specific meaning in this document. The original author licenses the intellectual property under terms that, in the page’s account, allow anyone to make and retain indefinitely many copies, whether changed or unchanged. Anyone can distribute or sell those copies, edit them, and claim authorship of copies. The fifth part of the definition concerns time. Once the author has released the work publicly under these terms, the page treats its open-source status as permanent. These provisions describe karbytes’ framework, including its unusually broad treatment of authorship claims.

A four-step hypothetical examines the conflict that can arise within that framework. Person A creates an original work called X and initially controls access to it. A then designates X as public domain, allowing anyone to copy it and treat their copies as their own property. Person B makes a copy called Y and initially controls that instance. B subsequently declares Y private and accuses A’s X of being an unauthorized copy of Y, reversing the actual order of creation. In the hypothetical, A retains the authority to possess, distribute, and assign access terms to copies A holds because A created X. The hypothetical also grants B authority to require permission to copy the particular instance called Y. The two claims can therefore be made about instances whose contents are indistinguishable, even though their histories differ.

Karbytes presents the example as an argument about the futility of enforcing copyright over reproducible information. The page’s practical conclusion concerns what creators should do with their own original material. It favors public-domain release as a way to make work available to as many information-processing agents as possible and to increase the chances that the work will circulate and survive. The thought experiment gives the page a reason to prefer broad release even when someone else might make a competing claim about an identical copy.

PUBLIC_DOMAIN is defined more narrowly than OPEN_SOURCE within the page’s vocabulary. It refers to open-source intellectual property that the page regards as legally ownerless after a specified date and as part of the public World Wide Web from that date onward. For files in the two sites, karbytes sets that point at the moment of public Web publication. The page also describes a preservation practice. During its functional lifetime, karbytes routinely saves files to the Wayback Machine to improve the likelihood that the public can retrieve the versions it intended as final drafts. Publication sets the terms in the page’s framework, while archiving is the recurring work of keeping those versions available.

A footnote extends the discussion of reproducibility to the identity of the author. The name Kar Beringer leads to an identification page that references plain-text files containing the author’s human genome. The author declares its own genome public-domain intellectual property and considers the possibility that another organism could have the same genome. The page initially presumes that exactly one organism with that genome is named Kar Beringer and is the original proprietor of both sites. It then qualifies that presumption with a speculative question about identical parallel universes and noumena. If indistinguishable instances can coexist in such a framework, the page suggests, there might be infinitely many concurrent instances of what appears phenomenally distinct, whether or not the noumenon has a corresponding appearance. The footnote thus distinguishes a reproducible description from the history by which a particular individual is identified.

The last section explains how hyperlink colors classify destinations across the two sites. Black, hexadecimal #000000, is the general background convention for direct access to raw files in public repositories under karbytes’ primary GitHub account, @karlinarayberinger. A green background, #00ff00, marks a nonraw GitHub page associated with either @karlinarayberinger or @karbytessupporter, including GitHub Pages destinations. For links on black backgrounds, the text color refines the classification. Green text may indicate a raw code file, a page on either WordPress site that karbytes considers more code-oriented than prose-oriented, or a Karbytessupporter Blogspot page that lists repository files for archival use. First-person commentary

My attention keeps returning to the vertical center. When the colors change quickly, the center gives me a way to recognize where I was looking a frame earlier. A small diamond can hold its position while nearly everything about its visibility changes. When its outline becomes the brightest thing nearby, I see it as an object laid over the field. When the surrounding cells brighten, I see the same diamond as a hole between them. I find that reversal more absorbing than the sheer saturation. It makes me reconsider a shape I thought I had already located.

The thinner passages make me want to follow one line around a polygon and see where it leads. It can trace a ring on one side, then become part of an X or a neighboring enclosure. Each interpretation lasts about as long as I can keep one scale of the pattern in view.

In the synchronized reconstruction, I imagine the bass pulse giving me a slower unit of time to inhabit while the colors change around it. The stretch near 15 seconds is where that relationship interests me most. The low layer recedes while the paired diamonds stay sharply defined. I can imagine feeling more space around the shapes during that break. As the low accents return and the regions between the contours fill with colored points, the same picture seems to acquire weight. A similar separation occurs late in the clip, where a vigorous palette cycle carries on through the bass reduction. I would expect my attention to alternate between tracking a beat and trying to hold onto a figure through a succession of color changes.

I also notice how much the fixed black bands contribute. They give the image two edges that I can trust even when the inner pattern appears to approach or recede. With those edges as a reference, the change of scale feels like something happening within the material of the picture. The view never pulls back to reveal what surrounds it. At the final cut, I still have the expectation that the last set of capsules will reorganize into another cluster. The cutoff feels like the removal of a view onto a process that could continue.

Seen alongside the yellow-box drawing, the clip makes me think about how differently a form can repeat. In the drawing, I can take my time tracing the same construction at three scales. In the video, a form can keep approximately the same place and yet seem to become a new object every time its surroundings change color. An exact copy of the MP4 preserves all the frames, their order, and their timing. What I carry forward from any one frame is much less exact. I remember a central opening, a pair of rings, or an impression of depth, and that fragment shapes what I see in the next frame. The file preserves the whole sequence. My attention keeps choosing which parts of it to carry into the next moment.
