The floating-point AAC decode contains peak samples slightly above nominal full scale, with the largest on the left. The file contains no separate captions or chapters.

I found no embedded **EXIF**, XMP, GPS, camera make or model, or camera capture settings. The MP4 movie and track creation and modification fields are zero. The date is given by the filename and corroborated by the outer desktop and the live keyboard log. For example, the initialization timestamp shown in the keyboard console converts to **November 5, 2026 at 5:32:35.818 p.m. Pacific Standard Time**. That timestamp belongs to the pictured application event.

## First-person commentary

The second clock is what changes my reading of the opening. At first I see a familiar OBS window over a dark browser page. Then the window moves away and there are two docks, two tab bars, and an earlier time inside the later one. I have to decide which pointer movement is happening now and which is already a recorded event. Once VLC goes behind Firefox, its picture disappears but its soundtrack carries on. I find that the earlier recording starts to feel like an active part of the current desktop, even when it has no visible window.

The keyboard gives me a different kind of motion to follow. Almost everything stays still, and a rectangle of green briefly becomes the most important thing on screen. A pair of keys can look like two small movements, one following the other. In the reconstructed sound, those movements can occupy a longer interval together. I find myself expecting the second member of a familiar pair before it lights up, then noticing a third flash or a longer hold that changes the grouping. The console below gives the gesture a frequency and a time in milliseconds. My impression of the gesture is less exact. I retain something like a lift in pitch, a repeated pair, or a pause that seemed longer because I had begun to expect the next one.

When the underlying replay drops away around 2:49, I can better distinguish the keyboard’s role in the mix. Its pitches have clean, almost rectangular stretches of time compared with the changing attack and decay of the guitars. The pitches can sound closely packed even while the green flashes leave plenty of gray between them. I imagine my attention moving between what the hand would be doing on a physical keyboard and what the app actually displays. The screen supplies the change of state. The tone has a life beyond that visible instant.

The first apple-game attempt produces an abrupt change in how waiting feels. On a dark loading tab, I expect the next image to replace it. Once the blue field is on screen, I expect each red disk to continue down. When the disks hang near the top and the timer remains at four seconds, the stopped movement is easier to feel than a blank page would be. The guitar waveform keeps moving underneath. Later, the game-over alerts also dim the field, but the empty canvas and the completed count give that similar dark overlay a different meaning. The first interrupts an expected fall. The later ones arrive after there is nothing left to fall.

During the working rounds, I find it hard to watch the apples evenly. My attention keeps moving to the lowest few and comparing their horizontal positions. The upper ones become a kind of forecast. If two low disks are far apart, I can already see that the next basket movement will favor one of them. A catch chime draws attention to the choice that worked, while a disk that slips past the bottom leaves a small visual gap. When VLC reaches the end of a cycle in the first round, the sharper sounds become easier to count as separate events. Then the guitars return and the same events become accents within a busier mix.

The end leaves me looking at the ordinary desktop details again. The game is finished, the last score has been acknowledged, and the basket sits near the left edge of a pale blue rectangle. OBS covers most of it, with a tiny version of the scene still visible in its preview. The replay carries sound from the earlier desktop right up to the boundary of the later recording. At the last frame, the pointer and the recording control are the only things I expect to act next. The game field has already come to rest.
